Ex-GTA dev believes Rockstar won’t be adding anything new to GTA 6 right now

Fans in Japan and Australia have flagged concerns about those versions potentially being censored, seeing as there is a notable difference in the size of their game files compared to everywhere else.

The preload period for GTA 6 is open to everyone at this point now, but it has caused some disruption for select internet providers in the UK.

#10604. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."

Materials provided by University of Illinois Grainger College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10602. Microsoft now lets you turn off AI search results completely in Bing

Microsoft is giving Bing users the option to turn off AI-generated answers in their search results, allowing them to browse the internet without being shown AI summaries.

AI has become a major part of online searches in recent years, with companies like Microsoft and Google using the technology to generate quick answers to questions instead of simply providing links to websites.

While this can be useful for quickly finding information, not everyone wants AI to be involved in their searches at all times.

Now, Microsoft is giving people more control over how they use Bing, with two simple ways to remove AI-generated answers from their results.

In a November 11 announcement, Microsoft revealed a new browser extension called Bing AI Search Choice, which lets users switch AI-generated answers on or off.

Once installed, the extension adds a toggle that can be used to choose between traditional search results and AI-powered summaries.

The setting applies to all searches made through Bing, meaning users won’t have to change their preferences every time they look something up.

However, the extension is currently only available for Google Chrome and Microsoft Edge, and installing it will also make Bing your default search engine.

For those who don’t want to install anything, simply adding “-ai” to the end of a Bing search will remove AI-generated answers from the results. For example, searching for “best games of 2026 -ai” will bring up results without the usual AI summaries.

Microsoft explained that the feature is about giving people the freedom to decide how they want to search.

New Microsoft patent could use AI to alter speech and lip movements in real time

How to turn off AI in Google search results

Bing AI is now available to everyone and it just got smarter

“AI is doing powerful things for search, and our research shows users want to have the option to choose whether or not AI is involved in every session,” the company said.

“Sometimes a quick AI-generated summary is exactly what you need. Other times, you’d rather scan the results, compare sources and decide for yourself what information to trust.”

The browser extension is currently in preview, meaning Microsoft could make further changes based on user feedback.

#10597. Alan Turing's biggest AI assumption may have been wrong

Alan Turing's famous ideas about artificial intelligence may have sent AI research down the wrong path for the past 75 years, according to prominent computer scientist Peter J. Denning.

In his new book, Turing's Mistake: Escaping the Yoke of Unintelligent Machines, Denning argues that two foundational assumptions made by Turing in 1950 continue to shape AI research today. The first is that intelligence can exist independently of a physical body and therefore be recreated in computer software. The second is that a machine can demonstrate intelligence by successfully imitating a human in conversation, an idea that later became known as the Turing test.

"These two claims have shaped much of AI research and development," Denning writes. "My premise is that our acquiescence to these claims has led to the AI mess in which we find ourselves today."

Denning argues that pursuing artificial general intelligence (AGI), or machines with human level intelligence, is unlikely to succeed. Instead, he warns, the technologies society is building could introduce significant new risks.

At the heart of Denning's argument is the idea of tacit knowledge, the vast amount of human understanding that cannot easily be put into words or represented in a form that computers can process.

He says machine learning cannot capture five major categories of tacit knowledge: common sense, everyday interactions with people and the environment, emotions and perception, practical performance skills, and the social and historical knowledge embedded in culture.

Researchers have long attempted to organize common sense into databases. One of the best known efforts was Douglas Lenat's Cyc project, which began in the 1980s with the goal of creating an extensive collection of common sense facts. After four decades of work, the project contained roughly 25 million entries.

"Yet even this treasury could not add up to a background of common sense sufficient to make expert systems smart enough to be experts," Denning notes. "Cyc validated that much of the knowledge that makes people experts cannot be articulated as propositions."

Denning believes practical skills present an even greater challenge.

"Our performance skills in thousands of domains cannot be communicated to machines," he explains. "Whereas descriptions of skillful outcomes ('know what') can often be represented as bits and stored in a machine, we do not know how to encode the embodied knowledge for skillful performance ('know how')."

He points to accomplished musicians as an example.

"A virtuoso violinist can play beautiful music yet cannot describe to an acolyte how to produce it.

"Even if a robot could observe and imitate skilled humans, having no biological body, a robot cannot grasp how the musician feels when playing beautiful music or how an audience feels when hearing it."

Denning also includes intuition, gut feelings, imagination, and spontaneous creativity among the forms of tacit knowledge that remain beyond the reach of machines.

Denning argues that all of these limitations stem from what he calls the "representation problem."

Computers can only perform calculations using data and instructions that have been encoded into physical forms they can recognize and process. Tacit knowledge, however, does not naturally fit into that framework.

"Behind every word is a deep well of tacit knowledge that gives it meaning," Denning says. "Words are but symbolic representations of meanings, not the meanings themselves. Commonly used Large Language Models, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini only manipulate words, they cannot know or understand the meaning of what they are saying."

According to Denning, this creates a fundamental divide. Because scientists still cannot fully explain how tacit knowledge works in humans, they also cannot translate it into a form machines can use.

"How we host tacit knowledge is largely a mystery," Denning admits. "All we know is that it is embodied. We have no idea what we might observe and measure in our bodies to reveal it."

Denning also argues that intelligence depends heavily on context, the surrounding circumstances that give words, actions, and decisions their meaning.

Context allows people to recognize sarcasm, humor, sincerity, and emotion. It helps determine when to be diplomatic, when to joke, and how to interpret countless social cues.

"When you inquire into where an assumption of the current context came from, you discover it rests on previous conversations from previous contexts. Each of those in turn rests on further previous conversations and their contexts. This pattern is endless and fractal," Denning explains.

Culture presents another major obstacle for AI.

Denning describes culture as encompassing values, norms, judgments, history, communities, moods, and even relationships involving power and care.

"Human conversations are imbued with background assumptions that give meaning and relevance to the words being used," Denning explains.

"Scaling up LLMs with ever larger neural networks will not enable them to acquire the embodied human knowledge we call culture. LLMs will not attain the objective of the Turing test: to demonstrate machine thought indistinguishable from human thought."

AI Safety and the Limits of Human Understanding

Denning concludes that humans and AI systems may ultimately develop different forms of tacit knowledge that neither can fully understand.

"Machines cannot read our tacit knowledge and we cannot read theirs," he writes. "We are aliens across an uncrossable divide."

He argues this gap raises serious concerns about AI safety. If machines cannot interpret the unspoken context behind human intentions, reliably aligning advanced AI systems with human goals may prove impossible.

"Through AI automation, agentic networks of machines are likely to develop their own machine intelligence that does not reach the level of human general intelligence but is still quite capable of creating severe problems for humans. This threat is a greater than a take-over by superintelligent machines," he explains.

"Machine intelligence has different concerns from us and does not appear to care about us. Its ways of thinking and problem-solving look alien to us. We do not yet know how to live safely with these machines.

"Pulling back from an AI automation singularity will demand much from us. We start by accepting that the familiar culture is fading away as intelligent machines appear in our society and we do not know what is coming. We decline to think like machines or be subservient to machines. We refuse to submit to a yoke imposed by low-intelligence machines. Most importantly, we reassert our humanity, declare once again what makes us different from machines, and celebrate those differences."

Materials provided by Taylor & Francis Group. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10595. Popeyes celebrates GTA 6 launch with ‘Spice City’ sandwich packed with double chicken

Popeyes is getting in on the GTA 6 hype with a new ‘Spice City’ sandwich featuring two crispy chicken fillets, arriving just in time for Rockstar’s biggest launch in years.

Grand Theft Auto 6 is finally set to release on November 19, 2026, and the excitement has reached the point where millions of Americans are reportedly planning to call in sick just to spend more time in Vice City.

Of course, anyone planning to spend their entire day playing GTA 6 is going to need something to eat, and Popeyes has decided to capitalize on the occasion.

The fast food chain has announced its new Spice City Sandwich, a limited-time offering inspired by the game’s fictional city and designed for players who have absolutely no intention of leaving their consoles.

The Spice City Sandwich features two of Popeyes’ signature crispy chicken breast fillets stacked on a buttery toasted brioche bun, complete with barrel-cured pickles and a choice of classic or spicy mayo.

Essentially, it’s the chain’s iconic chicken sandwich with twice the chicken, which should help players survive a few extra hours of causing chaos in Leonida.

“This new sandwich pays homage to the one you’ll be grabbing in the game, with a crave-worthy duo of chicken breast fillets packed in one sandwich. Between missions, order the Spice City Sandwich online or on the Popeyes app, and have it waiting at your door when you put your controller down,” the chain says.

According to a press release shared with Dexerto, the sandwich will be available nationwide in the US from November 19 through November 22, giving fans four days to get their hands on one.

GTA’s Burger Shot & Cluckin’ Bell are now real in Miami ahead of GTA 6

Interestingly, Popeyes isn’t the only fast food chain getting involved in the GTA 6 festivities.

As we previously reported, Rockstar has brought GTA’s fictional Burger Shot and Cluckin’ Bell restaurants to life in Miami, giving fans the chance to eat at the same restaurants they’ve been visiting in-game for years.

The promotion has already sparked a bizarre resale market, with fans paying hundreds of dollars for Burger Shot bags and other merchandise.

Between companies giving employees time off to play GTA 6 and restaurants preparing to feed them, November 19 is increasingly starting to look like an unofficial national holiday.

And if you’re planning to spend four straight days in Vice City, at least you won’t have to visit Cluckin’ Bell to get your chicken fix.

#10594. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (2026) review

I have a friend who first played The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time back in the day, when the game was only just released and he was home from university for Christmas. This is objectively the best of all times to play any video game: not Christmas specifically, but dropped out of the regular world and faced with a few weeks filled with nothing pressing to do whatsoever. Years later, though, my friend told me that the magic of the game didn't just come down to the playing of it, but the way that the playing of it unfolded.

Here's what he meant. He'd play all day, curtains drawn, family ignored, working through dungeons and pelting across Hyrule Field on Epona. Then in the evening he'd call his girlfriend, who was back with her own family for Christmas. She always wanted to know what he'd got up to that day. And not in East Sussex; she wanted to know what he'd got up to in Hyrule.

So as he played he took regular breaks to tell someone else about what he had encountered, and what he had discovered, and what he had yet to overcome. It is my bright delight today to be able to tell you that, around thirty years later, having just revisited the game in Nintendo's new remake, Ocarina of Time is still worth telling people about. It's still an adventure so surprising, so satisfying, so moving that I need to talk about it.

Here's the thing, though. Despite the game being a cast-iron masterpiece, this reaction was something of a surprise to me. Or rather, it was a surprise precisely because the game was a cast-iron masterpiece. In truth, a couple of weeks ago I wasn't really aching to head back to Ocarina of Time after all these years. I kind of dreaded having to slog through an echoing, once-great game and summon a thousand words of measured reverence afterwards.

Thankfully I had forgotten two things. The first is that I am an idiot, which means that I had failed to realise that any chance to replay a Zelda should be leapt at. And secondly, it turns out that I'd forgotten exactly what kind of masterpiece Ocarina of Time is. And along with that last point - and we'll get to this properly in a bit - I hadn't realised that there's a secret ingredient to this specific remake that has proved absolutely essential to its greatness, and that has nothing at all to do with the code running on the Switch 2.

Let's deal with remake duties here. This is more or less the game that people played back in 1998. The beats of the adventure, with Link travelling between different eras of Hyrule in order to defeat Ganon, remain the same. You still start in Kokiri Forest and you still ride Epona over the fence at Lon Lon Ranch in one of the great heart-racing, skin-prickling moments in all of art. If you're stuck in a dungeon, you can almost always use an existing guide from the N64 game to work your way through it. The game world is still broken up into little chunks that fade out and in as you move between them. And each game still begins with that unskippably evocative intro that has Link and Epona dashing over a misty hill with the moon behind them, Zelda meets Susan Cooper.

Changes though? For one thing the whole game's been updated with modern graphics, and has ditched any and all fixed cameras for a modern system of movement on one thumbstick and camera on the other. One example: Hyrule Castle Town, in my memory at least, always had a central camera and you would pivot about its streets as if you were a horse on a circus merry-go-round.

Now it's pure Frozen territory, cobbled paths racing off in every direction and you can explore them with the camera rushing behind you. The game's spaces feel less like elaborate machines that you were a mechanical part of, and more like video game spaces. Yes, from the way I've phrased that I now see that something of a trade-off has taken place here. I loved that sense you got in early 3D games that the whole thing was a clever practical trick that was forever almost about to collapse in on itself, but anyway! The new Ocarina is a game you can show kids who weren't alive in 1998 and they will feel at home more quickly.

Sometimes the graphical update is really wonderful. The route to Zora's Domain, which I barely remember in the original Ocarina, is now a lovely scramble over cliffs and arcing crags. A bold smattering of 1990s Nickelodeon colours and glossy textures, meanwhile, makes Jabu Jabu's Belly a cross between a pre-millennium rave and a modern soft play.

My favourite locations of all, however, are two of the mid-game dungeons. The Water Temple is now a drowned Art Nouveau palace that you could easily imagine Captain Nemo knocking around in. And the Forest Temple, which is my absolute favourite Zelda dungeon of all time, is a parquet-floored murder mansion that's been overtaken by trees. Dark wood-panelled corridors and weird bits of church sticking out of the foliage. It's the kind of place you'd hear a weird crime podcast about.

Trade-offs, however. My wife, a huge fan of the original game, would wander in and out as I played, and often felt the game she remembered was a little more characterful in its visuals. The moment you fight Dark Link, for example, has lost a little of its glitchy liminality, although the staging, with an old tree in the middle of a vast smoky room, is still wonderfully strange. Towards the end of the game, with the trip into Gerudo territory, you could also occasionally be forgiven for wondering if you'd stepped into one of a dozen third-person Unreal games in development at the moment. It's pleasant to look at, but it sometimes doesn't look specifically like Zelda anymore.

All that, and some strange character models that inevitably I learned to love, don't really matter very much, however. Not least because a handful of clever tweaks elsewhere mean that Ocarina now races along at an astonishing clip. And all this with a careful paring back of intrusions from Navi the fairy and the owl, who were both there in the original game to help move you along swiftly in the first place.

These tweaks are all pretty great. Link now has a jump button, which removes the original game's contextual jumps from the equation, and makes the thundering jump attack even more pleasing to pull off. Even better, in a loan from the 3DS remake of Ocarina, you can swap out usable items using a trigger mini-menu, and you can change boots and whatever you had on your head with some of the directional face buttons.

This doesn't sound like much, but it's quietly transformative in the way that the best updates are. Take the Water Temple. This is the stuff of memes in terms of difficulty and the patience required in navigating an environment that can be flooded up to three different levels. But now, as on the 3DS, you can enjoy it without having to slip into the menu every few steps to add your iron boots or remove them.

And this in turn means that a dungeon I had been really dreading turned out to be a dungeon that revealed itself via experimentation. I would change the water level and see what options I now had, think about the relationship between spaces I could reach and spaces I could not, all without needing to muddle about in menus and interrupt the flow of things. Flow is the word in fact: a simple UI and item-management tweak has given a legendary Zelda dungeon its perfect pace and sense of revelation.

Alongside tweaks like that which I noticed - oh, it's easier to equip the Hookshot - are no doubt hundreds of little things I didn't notice. I suspect there's a lot more gentle direction prompting built into places like the Water Temple, while elsewhere, in the Forest Temple, for example, I suspected that some of the backtracking had been removed. Individual item placements have been changed around perhaps, and there are generous tweaks like a wallet that holds more rupees from the off. For me at least, all of these tweaks had several things in common: they improved the game without removing or altering anything that was particularly meaningful about the original, and, also, I struggled to confidently spot them.

I did try to spot them for a while. And I measured up what I was playing against my memory, which is always wonky in the first place. Hyrule Field feels smaller now, but it's still thrilling to race across. I remember Epona entering the game sooner, or maybe I just remember the idea of a horse in a Zelda game being a huge, attention-holding deal. I thought a favourite boss involving oil paintings came much later, but again, I had just shifted it to the third act because of its original impact on me.

But this gives way over time to the most important thing about Ocarina of Time, I think, and the reason why this remake has such an emotional impact on people like me. This isn't just another Zelda with an ingenious overworld. It's a Zelda game that's even more preoccupied with time than ever before. And this is because time unfolds within the game, via a central mechanic that takes Hyrule seven years into the future, transforming Link from a child to an adult - and then allows you to go back again.

To play a game like this, a game that is about this stuff, after a period of almost thirty years away, gives your imagination and memory a kind of IMAX treatment. I realised that not only is this a game about the passage of time and the way that the world looks different when you're young to when you're a bit older - I had never really realised that adult Link goes through variations of the same dungeons that young Link went through, but now they're all just a bit harder and more consequential - it's also the kind of game that a lot of players have knowingly or unknowingly measured parts of their lives by.

This time, when I reached the first Fairy Fountain, for example, I remembered that my wife had walked down the aisle to this music - about half the registry office recognised the tune with a shiver of laughter, incidentally. Then there's the Water Temple, which has become cultural shorthand for a challenge that is really not screwing around. How many awful things had I faced in life by first conjuring the spirit of Link about to enter this place.

Players can change camera rotation speed and toggle motion-control aiming. Targeting can be controlled by holding a button or toggling a button, Ocarina can be controlled by the mic or by buttons, controller vibration can be switched on or off.

There's that first arrival in Hyrule Field, which is both a promise of everything that games were about to become in 1998 and that fleeting moment in childhood where the boundaries suddenly change. This game doesn't just send me back into my memories, then. It puts me back in conversation with all the people I have known who have ever loved it. I play it now and think I'm talking to my friend who played it at university, or my wife who loved it long before we met.

Was it always this good? Were the dungeons always so complex and challenging, but also so playful, so coherent? Were the bosses always driven by interesting ideas and drama and humour rather than difficulty? Was the world always so rich with minor characters who linger in the mind - the accordion player in the windmill, the gravedigger who has his own tour he'd really like you to try out? It's impossible for me to say at this point. This is Ocarina as it's always been, as timeless and intricate, but it also has a vibrancy and clarity that makes it feel like it was just created last Wednesday. If you're new to Zelda and you're coming to this game fresh, you're going to love it. Someone, somewhere, is about to play a game so special for the first time that they need to call up their friend and tell them all about it. But if you're returning to this game? Well, somehow you'll find that you're still coming to it fresh.

My dad always said that you should read Don Quixote three times in your life, and I only recently realised that he meant that the book changes because you change. So. When I got to the Deku Tree back at the start of Ocarina a few weeks ago, and I completed the dungeon and watched the impossible vision of the Deku Tree dying of weariness? I remembered that the first time I'd been here, almost thirty years ago, I couldn't help but identify with Link, freshly gifted a sword and shield, one dungeon down, ready for whatever came next. This time, I think, I was closer to identifying with the Deku Tree.

And here's the important part: this wasn't a depressing thought. It just meant that I had taken the long route to reach this point, and this astonishing game had taken the long route too. And yet we arrived at this point together, and together we were transformed. And that's really why any of this video game stuff matters.

A copy of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time was provided for this review by Nintendo.

#10591. New programmable photonic chip can control how fast light moves

Researchers at Seoul National University and the University of Seoul have developed a programmable photonic integrated circuit that can slow light whenever needed.

The team was led by Professors Namkyoo Park and Sunkyu Yu of the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at Seoul National University, working with Professor Xianji Piao of the School of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the University of Seoul.

Slowing Light Could Help Solve a Computing Bottleneck

The rapid growth of generative AI and large scale AI models has sharply increased the amount of computing power required by data centers and servers. Conventional electronic semiconductors are struggling to keep pace because they consume large amounts of energy and face limits in how quickly they can transmit data.

These challenges have intensified interest in optical computing, which uses light rather than electrical signals to process information. Optical systems could potentially move data at extremely high speeds while using less power.

However, light also presents a major challenge. Because it naturally moves at a fixed speed, it is difficult to delay optical signals or temporarily hold them in place. Those capabilities are essential for creating buffers and memory functions in optical computers.

To address this problem, the researchers designed a programmable photonic circuit that can control both the speed and shape of optical signals. Their approach provides more flexibility over "slow light" than previously proposed methods.

The study was published in the renowned international journal Advanced Science.

Why Optical Signals Sometimes Need To Wait

Photonic integrated circuits are emerging as a promising technology for processing information quickly and efficiently with light. In data centers, optical communication networks, and future computing systems, moving signals rapidly is only part of the challenge.

Systems must also ensure that different signals arrive at the correct time. In some cases, a light signal must be delayed so it can remain synchronized with other information moving through the system.

One method for creating these delays relies on coupled-resonator-induced transparency (CRIT), which uses interference among several optical resonators.

CRIT allows light within a selected frequency range to pass through a device while also reducing the speed at which the optical signal travels.

Traditional CRIT devices usually have operating characteristics that become permanent once they are manufactured. This makes it difficult to change how they function after fabrication.

For example, engineers who want to create a longer signal delay or work with a different frequency range often need to design and manufacture an entirely new photonic device.

That lack of adaptability increases the complexity of optical communication hardware and data center infrastructure. It can also raise costs and extend development schedules whenever new capabilities are needed.

The problem is especially important for AI servers and next-generation data centers, where enormous amounts of information must be processed in real time. Fixed optical components have therefore remained a major obstacle to more practical optical computing systems.

A Programmable Design for Controlling Light

The research team developed a different strategy by treating two optical states in CRIT systems, known as the bright mode and dark mode, as one unified degree of freedom. The researchers also added two controllable loop couplers.

Together, these changes created a new design principle for programmable photonic integrated circuits. Resonator arrangements that were previously locked into one configuration after fabrication could instead be adjusted for different purposes.

Using the new CRIT structure, the researchers showed that the movement of light could be delayed and controlled as needed. They also demonstrated that interference between the bright and dark modes could be handled as a single integrated design parameter.

This approach greatly expanded the flexibility of photonic resonator circuits that had previously been limited by fixed designs.

Controlling Delay, Bandwidth, and Signal Shape

The researchers theoretically demonstrated that the two loop couplers could be used to adjust the bandwidth and shape of the passband. They could also control how long signals were delayed and how efficiently those signals traveled through the circuit.

This means that both the speed and transmission behavior of optical signals could be reconfigured across entire systems containing multiple resonators, rather than only within a single resonator.

Numerical simulations also showed that the speed of optical pulses could be adjusted dynamically while the circuit was operating.

The results indicated that signal delay times could be changed without reducing processing performance. The system could also convert the frequency of light without requiring additional specialized components.

Simulations Suggest the Chip Could Be Practical

The researchers used three-dimensional electromagnetic simulations to test whether the CRIT device could be built on a silicon nitride (Si3N4) photonic integrated circuit platform.

They also evaluated a range of real-world issues that could affect the device during manufacturing and operation. These included material losses, differences in resonator quality, backscattering, coupling fluctuations, phase errors in the loop couplers, and thermal crosstalk.

The simulations indicated that the proposed structure could continue to operate reliably under realistic conditions.

One Chip Could Perform Several Optical Functions

The study introduces a programmable photonic platform that can control both the timing and frequency properties of light signals in real time.

The design could overcome the limitations of conventional optical delay devices, which typically perform only fixed functions. It also suggests that several important capabilities could eventually be combined within one photonic circuit.

These functions include signal synchronization, adjustable delay lines, optical buffers, and frequency conversion.

The same design principles may also be useful beyond CRIT systems. The researchers believe the approach could be applied to a broad range of photonic circuits based on resonators, potentially providing a foundation for more adaptable optical signal processing technologies.

Potential Benefits for AI and Data Centers

If the technology is commercialized, a single programmable optical chip could perform several tasks, including controlling signal speed and switching between different functions.

In that sense, the chip could operate in a way similar to a software-defined system, with its behavior adjusted according to changing needs.

This flexibility could help data centers and AI servers process information more efficiently while reducing energy consumption.

Combining several signal processing functions on one chip could also make optical communication equipment and sensor systems smaller and less expensive.

Over the longer term, the technology could support industries that depend on extremely fast information processing, including autonomous driving, next-generation communications, and quantum technologies.

Researchers Plan Larger Programmable Photonic Systems

Professor Namkyoo Park, co-corresponding author of the study from Seoul National University, stated, "This research is significant in that it proposes a new design principle that allows the flow of light within photonic integrated circuits to be reconfigured as needed, greatly enhancing design flexibility. We plan to expand this technology toward large-scale programmable photonic integrated circuits based on silicon photonics and photonic AI technologies."

Co-first authors Dr. Seungkyun Park and Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae, who led the theoretical framework and numerical analysis, added, "Through this study, we realized that reinterpreting conventional photonic resonator physics from a different perspective can serve as a starting point for discovering new functionalities in photonic integrated circuits. We plan to further develop this research toward practical device implementation and experimental validation."

Dr. Seungkyun Park is affiliated with the InnoCORE PICORE Center at KAIST and is currently conducting research on photonic AI and quantum optics at the Photonic Systems Laboratory, Seoul National University.

Ph.D. student Beomjoon Chae is conducting research on programmable photonic integrated circuits at the Intelligent Wave Systems Laboratory, SNU.

The research received support from the Ministry of Science and ICT through the Innovative Research Center (IRC) program, the Basic Research Laboratory (BRL) program, and the Young Researcher Program.

Dr. Seungkyun Park also participated in the study with support from the InnoCORE program (PICORE Center).

Materials provided by Seoul National University College of Engineering. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10590. Man buys two-bedroom apartment for just $1.70 in China’s Black Friday-style sale

A man in China has purchased an apartment for just $1.70 during a shopping promotion event, and it’s just as absurd as it sounds.

Shopping sales usually mean saving money on a shiny new TV or picking up a discounted game or two, but one lucky man in China took money-saving to an entirely new level by purchasing a two-bedroom apartment for just $1.70.

The property was sold through Chinese bidding platform Alibaba Auction as part of the country’s Double 11 event, an annual shopping festival that’s similar to Black Friday.

Located in Heilongjiang province in northeastern China, the seventh-floor apartment measures 68 square metres (roughly 732 square feet) and has two bedrooms.

According to the South China Morning Post, bidding started at one yuan (around $0.15) and was capped at 11 yuan (around $1.65). While 1,677 bidders took part, it was an anonymous shopper from eastern China’s Jiangsu-Zhejiang region who managed to secure the property.

Despite landing a new home for less than a cup of coffee, the buyer revealed he has no plans to relocate. Instead, he intends to use the two-bedroom property as a place to stay during winter holidays.

While the man may have purchased the apartment for $1.70, he will need to pay around 2,000 yuan (approximately $300) in deed taxes to complete the title transfer. SCMP also cited a warning from Beijing Jinsu Law Firm, which said buyers considering ultra-cheap properties should check for issues such as unclear ownership, unpaid taxes, and outstanding fees.

Either way, securing an apartment for just $1.70 puts it right next to owning an entire Japanese castle for $64,000 (yes, this is a real thing).

#10589. Eight years ago the idea of The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel had me captivated, but can Bethesda recapture the magic?

Eight years ago, I sat at my desk enthralled. Bethesda had just released the first trailer for The Elder Scrolls 6, a then mysteriously nameless, locationless sequel to one of the biggest games of all time, and I reckoned I'd just found the secrets to both. It was going to be set in the region of Hammerfell, I reckoned. And the city of Sentinel was a crucial factor in reasoning why.

Admittedly, I'm maybe a little more horrified at my level of Elder Scrolls obsession than I am chuffed at any apparent clairvoyance. Ultimately it was a bit of a punt, launched off the back of a day's worth of fanatical hyperfocus and exactly the right amount of the old Gamer Network office's weapons-grade filter coffee. "There's not really a lot to go on here", I wrote, sagely, "but, if we had to, we'd plant our flag in Hammerfell as the location of The Elder Scrolls 6."

Indulge me a bit and let's retread the reasoning. First, three assumptions were required in order to make any kind of guess at all: The Elder Scrolls 6 would not be set in all of Tamriel; not set outside of Tamriel; and would be set somewhere vaguely close in the timeline to the events of Morrowind, Oblivion and Skyrim - so as to make that bit of scrabbly rock and ruined castle, visible in that now infamous first teaser, at least somewhat traceable with contemporary descriptions in the lore.

Then came a lot of narrowing down. Not Morrowind, Cyrodiil, Skyrim or the Summerset Isles, I decided, as we'd been to all of those too recently (I'll leave it up to you whether another eight years since Morrowind lets that one still count as remotely "recent", but still). And then not Valenwood, Elsweyr, or Black Marsh, thanks to the topography of the land in the trailer and the type of environment on show. That left High Rock and Hammerfell, the former of which we ruled out because of its relative size - on the small side for a presumably beefy new TES game - and more importantly, the architecture that seemed at odds with the sandy stone just barely visible in the structures of that first teaser.

And finally: Sentinel. The city was perfectly positioned - along a coast with the sea to the right, dry mountains in the foreground, warm stone ruins to the left, no visible land beyond. Now, that setting is all but confirmed - "all but" as, technically, nothing's set in stone yet, pardon the pun. All we've since been shown is a sand dune, an Elder Scroll, the name Sentinel, and a brief quote from a Moth Priest to a Hammerfell High Queen. But we can pretty much lock it in.

To the Elder Scrolls obsessive who spent that day eight years ago scurrying down endless YouTube rabbit holes and lingering on the dustiest unofficial wiki pages, the thought of a game set there ought to be tantalising. Already, even with that paper thin new teaser, there's enough to start picking at. The new trailer's quote is dated just four years after the events of Skyrim, for instance, with both that territory and Cyrodiil located right next door, making for some lovely opportunities for crossovers and references to wider events. And the quote itself is curious. As our guides team have already speculated, the notion of "A future lost. A victory undone. Sands of time scattered by fate" gives off the faintest whiff of a time-forking Dragon Break. That being the timeline-branching, multiple-player-choice-enabling thing that happened at the end of The Elder Scrolls 2: Daggerfall, a game which took place - aha! - in the Iliac Bay between High Rock and Hammerfell. Surely, the same bay we're looking at in that original TES 6 trailer.

It keeps going! The "victory" mentioned in that quote could refer to the victory won by the people of Hammerfell against a Thalmor invasion, in a conflict that - ooh! - took place around the same time Skyrim's main story was happening, and just a few years before that quote is dated. Could we get branching timelines? A tying together of the overarching Thalmor plotline that's been simmering away until now? A load of varying endings, as Bethesda experimented with so openly in Starfield? I'm off again. Fire up the Unofficial Elder Scrolls Pages!

There's such magic here, far beyond the apparent mastery Bethesda has over theory-baiting teasers for suckers like me. These games, for instance, also have an uncanny knack for joyful stupidity. There are Oblivion NPC barks out there that landed right as YouTube's golden era hit liftoff, and in turn defined an entire generation's sense of humour. I was once in a Slack channel here called #reeblereeblereeble, which was just myself and two other writers punting Imperial Watch taunts at each other to lighten the mood every couple of weeks, or whenever someone seemed a bit stressed. When I leave a gathering of old school friends I still catch myself bidding them "farewell!" with Wood Elf gusto, and giggling at the thought of being immediately squished into mulch.

And none of that is to mention the design itself, which has at times been more or less unparalleled. The clockwork, systemic magic of Morrowind is extraordinary, the first hour alone a lesson - at least as important as good old over-cited Mario 1:1 - in instructional design. Within a walkable radius of the musty little settlement of Seyda Neen Bethesda gave the player every tool necessary for understanding - and subsequently playing with, then outright breaking - its entire world. With Oblivion it brought the role-playing open world to the masses. With Skyrim it expanded what our very concept of the masses means.

By all accounts I should, then, be absolutely drooling at the thought of The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel. But for some reason the excitement is muted. It's been like this since long before this second teaser, frankly, and for much of that time I've put it down to Bethesda itself.

Bethesda Game Studios has not been on a good run. Fallout 76 might've finally recovered from its nightmarish launch, but time hasn't been hugely kind to Fallout 4, which longer-running fans have come to generally settle on as decent, but something of a slight step back from the wilder, more playful tendencies of Fallout 3 and Obsidian's New Vegas. The same could be argued of Skyrim, a wonderful game in its own right that did, nevertheless, sand down some of The Elder Scrolls' signature RPG elements into something smoother, almost closer to a frictionless modern action game (a topic on which Elder Scrolls fans have some rather strong opinions, though that's a whole other debate). And then there's Starfield, a perfectly alright game that, by virtue of that, is sort of a disaster. Its mere adequacy has only served to harden the belief, amongst long-running fans, that Bethesda has lost its magic touch.

The natural question, then, is can Bethesda recapture the magic? Again for some time I've been worried the answer might be 'no'. I worried about the BioWare-ification of the studio: the failed (albeit very well recovered) live service punt, the wonky new IP, the consolidation beneath changing owners, the shifting goalposts, the creeping corporate-ification and apparently inevitable entropy of big, creative RPG studios over a long enough period of time. The Bethesda of today is not the Bethesda of Morrowind, Oblivion or Skyrim. It's probably not even the Bethesda of Starfield. And worryingly, it's shown for some time now that it seems to care more for broad appeal - that mirage of financial success - over having caché with its long-running fans. That kind of blinkered thinking always comes back to bite.

But that's not it. I worried about that, but then I learned to stop worrying about that. The reality is Bethesda can absolutely recover the magic, just as any studio can - including BioWare for that matter, had it been given a clear, EA nonsense-free chance - for the obvious reason that no two development cycles need ever be the same. Development studios are not individuals, they're not sportspeople who go in or out of form, or see the talent fade as their legs start to give out. They're massive organisations full of all kinds of people. And so just as people leave, other people can join. There are always systemic issues at work, but you can overcome those if you can hire well, design well, plan well - if your owners are smart enough to let you, if you're given enough runway, and your leaders know what they're doing. Good game development, like just about anything done with a large team, is ultimately about making good decisions consistently over time. This is entirely possible for Bethesda to do, with the right people in charge to make them. That goes for the people above Bethesda's top brass, too.

Will Bethesda deliver? Yes, no, maybe. In a way it's besides the point. Instead, I've realised the thing that gives me pause, before burrowing giddily down into another warren of silly Tamriel backstory, is that the world this Elder Scrolls game will launch into will just be so thoroughly different to the last. Every video game from a western, triple-A publisher is life-or-death now. Every sales figure, metascore, or stray quote is another algorithmically driven hate-narrative, a collective Emperor's thumbs down. And every ultimate owner of these games now seems to be properly set on being really impossible not to loathe.

Bethesda can't make games that are just alright, because today alright means doom. It takes too long, for one - we're already well into GTA 6 territory here, 15 years on from Skyrim and counting, with master of whispers Jason Schreier suggesting at least another two to go. And by virtue of that, its games must surely cost too much. The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel will sell gangbusters, which will mitigate that. But at this level of spending, the level of the stratosphere, a sales hit alone arguably isn't enough. The world demands something more.

You take that long and cost that much and your game has to shake the mountains and rumble the earth. Move the industry or revive it. Sell physical consoles that are likely to cost into the low thousands. Rewrite a narrative of failure, incompetence, arrogance, waste, and decline. This is not me declaring those things necessary; it's the way of the industry now. The sands have shifted. A game like The Elder Scrolls 6 is now a symbol. In this case one of its owner, Xbox.

That company has no other cards to play but this one, as evidenced by CEO Asha Sharma jangling her Todd Howard-shaped keys every time the heat from all that delicious bloodletting gets a little much. Sentinel has to not only be successful, then, but widely adored. Anything else, after hundreds of millions on its development, billions on wider acquisitions, thousands upon thousands of layoffs (and, let's not forget, a boycott derived from still blood-splattered links to what a UN commission calls a genocide), will be a failure. Not a failure for Bethesda's developers, by any stretch, but for the company at large after the weight of context Xbox has dropped on top of them. A failure to flip over the table at the dying moment of a losing game. A failure for those who are all too desperate to change the story.

All this - all the perpetual, seemingly endless turmoil of making video games, the cynicism in the air of so many gaming spaces, the terminally compromised corporate ownership of the games themselves - is what makes excitement so hard to muster. I look at The Elder Scrolls now and can't help but feel a little tinge of anger or sadness. Anger that yet another route to open joy - and to unserious teen memories and, frankly, a tiny little part of a personal identity, no matter how hard I try to resist that - has become wound up with such unsavoury ownership. All this, reduced to yet another PR tool and distraction technique to shift eyes and words away from bleak and pointless cruelty. And then sadness, at the idea I might escape into it, whenever it finally arrives, only to find myself in exactly the world I wish I could just briefly leave behind. Bethesda's developers can recapture the magic. But can the world of video games let them?

#10586. Before Dark Souls there was King's Field, and five hours into brilliant homage Queen's Domain, it's already one of my favourite games this year

The clue, of course, is right there in the title. Developer Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain isn't just a retro-fantasy action RPG played in first-person; it's an explicit homage to King's Field, the long-dormant FromSoftware series that - as the precursor to Demon's Souls, Dark Souls, Bloodborne, Sekiro, Elden Ring, and all those other Soulslikes - helped shape the modern gaming landscape in its own small way. That, though, is pretty much where my knowledge of the series begins and ends - as much as I love FromSoftware, I've never actually played a King's Field game. I've no idea how Queen's Domain shapes up as Kings Field-like, then, but I do know that, five or so hours in, it's already one of my favourite games this year.

Queen's Domain begins, as many great games do, on a beach. Our hero, Prince Beriik, has been washed ashore after his expedition ship, on a voyage to the mysterious Isle of Neasied, is destroyed. Beriik is searching for his father, King Gius Abraon, who's vanished on a mission to save the kingdom of Bamaen - after years of peace, the evil Queen Siraatsu's army of the damned is stirring; the dead are rising, legendary beasts are returning, and the creatures of the land have turned feral. But for now, there's simply a beach, the cry of distant gulls, and the gentle breaking of surf upon the shore. Queen's Domain's lore might read like an explosion in a vowel factory, but you certainly can't fault its mood.

There's something, perhaps, of the superb Dread Delusion in Queen's Domain; specifically in the way both deploy the lo-fi video game aesthetics of the 90s and early 2000s to invoke a more deliberate kind mood: those unnatural low-poly angles, those shimmering lo-res textures, and those rasterised skies creating - when viewed en masse across an expansive interconnected world - a particularly woozy sort of landscape. Here, though, the vibe isn't so much putrescence as chilly isolation, the muted vistas awash in greens, greys, browns, and blues.

It's a mood sent further off-kilter by the wonderfully odd soundtrack, whose seeping retro synths shift unpredictably from ambient melancholy to thumping dissonance, drawing in everything from parping trumpets to quacking ducks. And then there's the geographic strangeness - the secretly labyrinthine shape of this deceptively compact island - that only grows more apparent the further you explore.

Once you pick yourself off the beach and wander inland, the path almost immediately forks: to the left a boarded trail, to the right a vast stone door. Up ahead a gated cave mouth, and looming high on the clifftop, a mysterious ancient tower. Queen's Domain is full of these kinds of curiosity piquing landmarks, serving as both orientation points and unspoken goals.

Queen's Domain isn't exactly what you'd call forthcoming, you see, and early on - in lieu of a more explicit objective - that tower seems like a promising lead. Rusty blade in hand, you plot a likely course, but while there's a sort of logic to the island from afar, the routes between its landmarks are woven in wonderfully convoluted ways. And before you know it, your path has spilled out onto the coast, winding a perilous cliffside route high above the crashing waves. A second option leads further inland, plunging downward into a shadowy glade. And it's only after battling an onslaught of ladybird-rat hybrids and unnerving chicken-crickets (Queen's Domain loves its weird wildlife amalgams) you realise the two routes converge.

From there, more branches, but always a tangled path; the island's deliberately disorientating design turning navigation and exploration into a fascinating puzzle. And that only increases once you find your first proper sword. Imbued with a powerful darting slash, it's both an offensive tool and - with a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency strafe - an occasionally defensive one. But, it also grants traversal powers, those darting thrusts carrying you across previously impassable gaps. Soon, exploration, initially restricted to ground level, grows increasingly vertiginous, opening up whole new possibilities. You'll bound across treetops, charting a circuitous path around the island, or return to those shattered bridges and see what lies beyond. And that 'what' has frequently managed to surprise me - at one point, an apparently simple ascent turns into a several-hour trawl through a water temple.

Paths aren't always obvious, either; you'll plunge into a pool of deadly miasma, shuttling forward at the last moment to propel yourself along a carefully concealed pipe, or investigate a previously unreachable overhang only to unearth a secret passageway leading to horrifying depths. And often, you'll stumble across sudden shortcuts, causing routes to unexpectedly fold back on themselves. Truthfully, the freedom is mostly an illusion - the island is carefully designed to prevent you from deviating too far - but the proliferation of secret corners, and the minimal handholding, means the paths you're forging feel like your own. And as you unlock new tools, you realise there's a Metroidvania aspect to these spaces too. It's clever stuff, particularly when more overt puzzles are introduced, feeding further into the traversal rhythms.

And throughout it all, combat serves as Queen's Domain's drumbeat, punctuating progress with encounters whose wilfully rudimentary systems do, admittedly, take a bit of getting used to. It's not just the deliberate wooliness of it all as Freshly Baked emulates the combat of video games yore, or enemies' merciless in-your-face lock-on once disturbed, it's the limited ways you can respond. You can swing your sword, step outside of an incoming attack (there's nothing so advanced as a dedicated dodge button), and, well, that's pretty much it. And even accounting for enemies' minimal, largely predictable attack patterns, you'll invariably fumble and die.

But slowly it evolves into something compelling; a methodical game of feints and opportunistic returns, of goading enemies into attacks so you can, as far as your stamina gauge allows, exploit the resulting window. It's still far baggier than, say, Dark Souls, but it finds an engaging rhythm. Partly that's down to a combat twist that actively encourages restraint: standard weapon swings barely do more than chip damage, but hold the button to charge up your attack, and you'll do substantially more harm. That turns combat into a dance of patient timing, as you try and find the perfect moment to unleash a swing as multiple enemies move in.

That's just the foundation though. Soon enough, you've got throwing knives (which slowly replenish over time) for kiting, supplementary damage, or picking off sentry-like foes at a distance. There are spears and bludgeons, often with unusual secondary abilities, and it all feeds into a rock-paper-scissors-style damage system that encourages experimentation with your arsenal - especially where bosses are concerned. And the final flourish is an automatic levelling system that doesn't just strengthen your character as you play, it levels individual weapons - eventually causing them to evolve. It encourages engagement with enemies rather than avoidance, and provides a regular sense of progress in a game where the sometimes unforgiving challenge means inevitable repetition.

Queen's Domain is not, I should stress, a Soulslike, despite some similarities. You don't drop stuff on death, meaning no desperate corpse runs, and while resting and replenishing your energy at crystals resets the world - encouraging some push-your-luck risk-taking - you can save your game independently, creating some welcome breathing room.

Put all this together and Queen's Domain is a wonderful surprise; a moody, sometimes silly retro throwback that, despite its self-imposed mechanical and aesthetic limitations, feels anything but limited. Five hours or so into its supposedly 40 hours of playtime, its mix of combat, exploration, and puzzling has settled into a properly compelling rhythm - and despite some tricky bits and my own cack-handed frustrations, it keeps pulling me back in again. I've no idea if Queen's Domain is a good King's Field homage, but so far at least, it's a bloody good time.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10584. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns amid spy controversy

Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang has resigned after 33 years leading the company, with an interim replacement taking over immediately.

The announcement on November 12 comes just four days after Nvidia launched an independent investigation into reports that Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence operation involving a suspected Chinese spy.

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia in 1993, has stepped down as president, CEO, and a member of the board of directors.

Nvidia confirmed that Colette Kress, its executive vice president and chief financial officer, will serve as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent replacement.

Kress joined Nvidia in September 2013 and has overseen its financial operations during a period of significant growth. She will retain her existing responsibilities while serving as interim CEO, with the company considering both internal and external candidates for the permanent position.

The leadership change follows reports that Huang had an extramarital relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based technology company Megaspeed, whom US intelligence officials reportedly suspected of working for Chinese intelligence.

The two are not related, and the allegations prompted Nvidia’s board to announce an independent investigation on November 8, overseen by outside legal counsel.

At the time, Nvidia said it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets had been compromised. The company also expressed confidence in Huang’s leadership and confirmed that he would cooperate with the investigation.

Huang subsequently acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees, apologizing to his family and colleagues while maintaining his commitment to the company.

The controversy also attracted attention from US lawmakers, with three House committee chairs requesting a closed-door briefing with Huang on November 18 to discuss his interactions with Alice Huang and Nvidia’s security procedures.

Nvidia launches independent investigation into CEO after reports of suspected Chinese spy link

Nvidia stopping RTX 5090 production to focus on AI graphics cards: Report

Nvidia CEO warns US of dangers surrounding Chinese chip war

China’s Foreign Ministry has rejected the espionage allegations, while Singaporean authorities have said they did not identify Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia’s November 12 announcement did not address the status of its investigation or whether Huang would continue cooperating following his departure.

Alongside Kress’s appointment, Nvidia announced that Harvey C. Jones would become the company’s first-ever chair of the board.

Jones, who has more than 30 years of experience in the technology industry, said the company expects its operations and product roadmap to continue without interruption.

“Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position,” Jones said.

“She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA’s strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company’s operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption.”

Kress also addressed her appointment, thanking Huang for his contributions to Nvidia over the past three decades.

“NVIDIA’s strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years,” she said.

“We will continue advancing the work that has made NVIDIA a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.”

Huang’s departure comes as Nvidia faces increased scrutiny over its leadership and business dealings with China, with its advanced AI chips remaining subject to US export restrictions.

The company is scheduled to release its third-quarter fiscal 2027 financial results on November 17, followed by an earnings call later that day. Nvidia confirmed both events will proceed as planned.

#10583. MIT’s new lidar chip could give self-driving cars a wider view

Lidar technology uses pulses of infrared light to calculate distances and create detailed 3D maps of the surrounding environment. This allows autonomous vehicles to detect objects in their path and respond quickly. However, conventional lidar sensors are often large and costly, and many rely on moving components that can wear out over time. These limitations make the systems difficult to use in a wider range of settings.

MIT researchers have now developed an approach that could lead to smaller, more durable lidar sensors that operate without any moving parts. Their advance centers on a new silicon-photonics chip, a type of semiconductor device that controls light instead of electrical signals.

Existing lidar systems built with silicon-photonics chips usually have a narrow field of view. As a result, they struggle to scan areas located toward the edges of a scene. Previous attempts to expand this viewing range have often introduced extra noise and reduced measurement accuracy.

The MIT team addressed those problems by creating an array of integrated antennas that greatly limits unwanted crosstalk, which occurs when neighboring antennas interfere with one another. The design allows the chip to scan across a broader field of view while producing less noise than other silicon-photonics-based methods.

The advance could support the development of more capable lidar sensors for challenging uses, including autonomous vehicle navigation, aerial mapping, and the monitoring of construction sites.

"The functionality we demonstrated in this work solves a fundamental problem for integrated optical-phased-array technology, enabling future lidar sensors that can achieve significantly higher performance than we could demonstrate previously," says Jelena Notaros, the Robert J. Shillman Career Development Associate Professor of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS) at MIT, a member of the Research Laboratory of Electronics, and senior author of a paper on this innovation.

The study also includes lead author and EECS graduate student Henry Crawford-Eng, along with EECS graduate students Andres Garcia Coleto, Benjamin M. Mazur, Daniel M. DeSantis, and Tal Sneh. The findings were published recently in Nature Communications.

Many traditional lidar systems use a large rotating unit to direct light pulses across a scene. When the light strikes nearby objects, it reflects back toward the sensor. The returning signals provide the information needed to reconstruct a detailed map of the environment.

Silicon-photonics-based lidar works differently. Rather than rotating a mechanical device, it scans a beam of light electronically in several directions with a system known as an integrated optical phased array (OPA).

At the heart of an OPA is a group of integrated antennas. Each antenna contains tiny, regularly spaced variations along its length. These features, known as corrugations, cause light from an input source to scatter upward and out of the photonic chip.

Researchers can control the direction of the outgoing beam by changing the phase of the light sent to each antenna. Adjusting these phases changes the angle at which the array releases light, making it possible to steer the beam without moving any physical components.

Placing the antennas close together creates a serious obstacle. Neighboring antennas can couple with one another, scrambling the light they produce. Engineers have traditionally prevented this interference by increasing the distance between antennas, but wider spacing creates a different set of problems.

When antennas are too far apart, the array produces several copies of the same beam at different angles. The primary beam can only be moved a limited distance before it becomes difficult to distinguish from these additional copies.

"This limits our field of view, so the autonomous vehicle now only knows what is in front of it for a certain angular range," Garcia Coleto explains.

The unwanted beam copies, called grating lobes, can confuse the sensor and generate false detections. They also consume energy that could otherwise be directed into the main beam.

To overcome this tradeoff, the MIT researchers developed antennas with reduced crosstalk that can be positioned close together without strongly coupling.

In a conventional OPA, every antenna has an identical structure and uses the same pattern of corrugations. When these matching antennas are placed close together, they interact very strongly.

The MIT team instead created a repeating set of three antennas with distinct shapes. They changed the width of the antennas as well as the size and placement of the corrugations. Because the antennas have different geometries, each one also has a different propagation coefficient, which describes how light moves through the structure.

"Because the antennas have very different propagation coefficients, when we put them close together, essentially each antenna doesn't 'see' the antenna next to it. Therefore, it won't couple with its neighbor," Garcia Coleto says.

Making Different Antennas Behave the Same Way

Reducing the coupling was only part of the challenge. Although the antennas needed different propagation coefficients, they still had to release light in the same consistent manner.

The team designed the antennas around three essential requirements.

Each antenna had to emit the same amount of light. Every antenna also needed to release its beam at the same angle when receiving the same wavelength. Finally, the angle of emission had to change evenly across the entire array as the beam was steered.

"We have this challenge where we require the antennas to have different geometries to reduce the crosstalk, but we need to simultaneously design the antennas to have the same emission characteristics. While it is possible to engineer this, it is extremely difficult because, typically, when antennas are designed with different geometries, they tend to behave differently," Crawford-Eng says.

The researchers began by developing the basic electromagnetic theory describing how radiative modes couple. They then used this theoretical framework to guide the design and computer simulation of the antennas.

Based on those calculations, the team manufactured an OPA containing the reduced-crosstalk antennas. The antennas were placed much closer together than those in a conventional system, and the completed device was then tested experimentally.

Interference Falls From About 100 Percent to 1 Percent

Under the conditions of the experiment, a typical OPA would have produced coupling of approximately 100 percent. The MIT design lowered that coupling to about 1 percent while still generating one clean and precise beam.

The system accurately steered the beam across a broad field of view without producing any grating lobes. This combination of wide scanning, low interference, and strong beam quality addresses one of the central obstacles facing integrated lidar technology.

The researchers now plan to refine the method so the system can cover an even broader viewing range. They are also studying another possible approach to wide field-of-view performance that emerged while they were developing the underlying theory.

"This work addresses a longstanding challenge in integrated optical phased arrays: simultaneously achieving both a wide field of view, which requires dense antenna spacing, and high beam quality, which requires low crosstalk between neighboring antennas. The authors solve this problem with an elegant antenna design. Their innovation is an important step forward for chip-scale, solid-state beam-steering technology," says Joyce Poon, professor of electrical and computer engineering at the University of Toronto and director of the Max Planck Institute of Microstructure Physics, who was not involved with this work.

The Semiconductor Research Corporation, the National Science Foundation, an MIT MathWorks Fellowship, the U.S. Department of War, and the MIT Rolf G. Locher Endowed Fellowship supported the research, in part.

Some of the work was carried out using MIT.nano facilities.
