#10581. How to Optimize AI-Generated Images for Faster Websites

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming a common part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product illustrations, landing pages, blog posts, and digital interfaces. However, generating an image is only one part of the process. Before publishing it, developers need to ensure that the image loads quickly, displays correctly across devices, and maintains its visual quality.

Large image files can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay the appearance of important content. These problems can affect user experience and website performance, particularly on mobile devices and slower internet connections.

A reliable image optimization workflow helps address these challenges. By selecting appropriate dimensions, choosing efficient image formats, automating image processing, and implementing responsive delivery, developers can improve loading performance without sacrificing visual quality.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques and development tools.

Images often account for a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. Uploading large, unoptimized images can make pages slower and increase the amount of data visitors need to download.

Several common problems contribute to poor image performance:

Incorrect dimensions waste bandwidth on smaller screens.

Missing image dimensions can cause unexpected layout shifts.

Poor responsive configuration can make browsers download unnecessarily large images.

Incorrect loading priorities can delay important visual content.

These issues can affect Core Web Vitals, including Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

For developers, image optimization should therefore be part of the website development workflow rather than a final task performed immediately before publication.

Before optimizing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small interface graphic.

For example, a website might use the following dimensions:

These dimensions are examples rather than fixed requirements. The appropriate size depends on the website layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

When preparing source visuals, developers can explore different AI image generation options and consider how the generated images will fit their intended layouts.

Once the image is ready, keep a high-quality master file. This allows developers to create smaller versions for different screen sizes without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Avoid enlarging small images unnecessarily because upscaling can introduce blur and visible artifacts.

Choosing the right format can reduce image size while preserving acceptable visual quality.

Three commonly used formats are AVIF, WebP, and JPEG.

AVIF: Often provides efficient compression for photographs and detailed illustrations. It can significantly reduce file sizes, although encoding may take longer.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation. It offers broad browser support and is suitable for many website images.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and situations where compatibility with older software or systems is important.

PNG is appropriate for certain lossless graphics, screenshots, and images that require transparency. However, it can produce unnecessarily large files when used for photographic content.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare file sizes and inspect visual quality before selecting the final output.

A practical approach is to generate AVIF and WebP versions and provide a JPEG fallback where necessary.

Manually resizing and converting images becomes time-consuming when a website contains dozens or hundreds of visual assets.

Automation makes the process more consistent. Developers can use Sharp, a Node.js image-processing library, to resize images, convert formats, and generate multiple versions from a single source file.

Next, create a file named build-images.mjs:

The script creates multiple image sizes in both WebP and AVIF formats.

The quality settings are starting points, not universal recommendations. Developers should adjust them after checking the resulting file sizes and visual details.

This approach is particularly useful for content-heavy websites, product catalogs, and applications that publish images regularly.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on the visitor's screen and the space available in the layout.

HTML provides two important attributes for this purpose: srcset and sizes.

The srcset attribute lists the available image files and their widths. The sizes attribute describes how wide the image is expected to appear at different viewport sizes.

In this example, the image is expected to occupy approximately 720 pixels on wider screens and nearly the full viewport width on smaller screens.

The browser uses this information to choose an appropriate image candidate.

One common mistake is setting sizes="100vw" for an image that occupies only a small part of the page. This can encourage the browser to download a larger file than necessary.

Always ensure that the sizes attribute reflects the actual website layout.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide multiple image formats and a fallback option.

The browser selects a supported format, while the img element provides the fallback image and alternative text.

Developers should verify that every referenced file exists and that its dimensions match the intended layout.

The picture element can also support different crops for desktop and mobile devices. This is useful when a wide banner needs a separate composition for smaller screens.

Images without defined dimensions can cause content to move while the browser loads the file. This behavior contributes to Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Including width and height attributes helps browsers reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

For responsive layouts, developers can use CSS:

This allows images to scale within their containers while maintaining their proportions.

Images below the initial viewport can generally use native lazy loading:

However, the main hero image should not normally be lazy-loaded when it is responsible for LCP. Delaying an important above-the-fold image can make the page appear slower.

Where appropriate, developers can use fetchpriority="high" for the main image to help the browser prioritize its download.

The correct strategy depends on the image's position and importance within the page.

Image optimization involves more than file size and dimensions. Developers should also consider accessibility, file organization, and metadata.

Alternative text helps people using screen readers understand meaningful images. It should describe the image's purpose in the context of the page.

Decorative images that provide no useful information should generally use an empty alt attribute.

File names should also be descriptive. Names such as home-office-sunrise.webp are easier to understand and maintain than generic names such as image-final-v3.png.

Metadata requires additional consideration. Image-processing tools may remove metadata by default to reduce file size. This can include information that a project needs to preserve, depending on its requirements.

Before removing metadata, developers should review whether the website or client requires attribution, provenance information, or other embedded details.

After implementing an optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Chrome DevTools provides useful information about image requests.

Open Developer Tools and select the Network panel.

Resize the viewport or use device emulation.

Check whether the browser downloads appropriately sized images.

Developers can also inspect the image selected by the browser through the console:

This returns the current image URL selected for that element.

For websites with multiple images, inspect the relevant elements individually.

Finally, run an audit using Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. These tools can identify issues involving oversized images, image formats, loading priorities, and other performance factors.

Image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, JavaScript, and third-party scripts can also influence loading speed.

Several mistakes can reduce the effectiveness of an image optimization workflow:

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one format always produces the smallest file.

Omitting the sizes attribute from responsive images.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Applying excessive compression and damaging visual details.

Removing metadata without checking project requirements.

Measuring file size without inspecting the final visual quality.

A consistent testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

No. AVIF often provides efficient compression, but the results depend on the image, encoding settings, and quality requirements. Comparing both formats is the best way to choose an appropriate option.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

Yes. Reducing the size of the main content image, providing responsive versions, and avoiding unnecessary loading delays can improve LCP when that image is the largest contentful element.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of the page should generally load promptly.

The fundamental techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, AI-generated visuals may contain complex textures, gradients, and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design and make it easier to create custom visual content. However, efficient delivery requires more than exporting an image and uploading it to a website.

Developers should choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive image delivery. Including image dimensions helps prevent layout shifts, while appropriate loading priorities can improve the experience for visitors.

By testing images across different devices and reviewing their actual performance, development teams can maintain visual quality while reducing unnecessary downloads.

A repeatable optimization workflow makes it easier to publish new visuals without sacrificing website speed, accessibility, or reliability.

#10580. In Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert doubles down on its maximalism in audacious, faintly sinister style

And so we are back in Pywel: the super-sized and often super-pretty realm where the deserts are blood-red, the grass is a lurid shade of green, and Kliff and his merry gang of Greymanes seem to be pissed all the time. Yet what's this? Peace seems to have broken out across the region, which means our grizzled action hero is in a reflective mood.

At the outset of Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert's recently released DLC, I'm tasked with taking Kliff out for a quiet spot of moonlit fishing. The problem is, I seem to have forgotten how to actually fish - and if memory serves, perhaps I never learned because the system was so poorly explained in the base game. So I flail around for a full 15 minutes before eventually relenting and consulting Reddit on how to snag a bite.

You might reasonably assume that these types of basic explanatory wrinkles had been ironed out in the countless updates since Crimson Desert's release in March. Certainly, developer Pearl Abyss has gone to great effort elsewhere to streamline what was, at times, a frustratingly clunky experience (those menus at launch, oh god) while adding even more stuff, such as all-new cutscenes (to improve the story's legibility) and the ability to ride wolves and tigers (because why the hell not). But for all the tinkering and wholesale additions, Crimson Desert has retained its essential character: the menus remain a total faff and the narrative, which I started afresh some weeks ago, is still bafflingly incoherent (maybe even more than I initially appreciated).

It's into this churn of ongoing development that Pearl Abyss has produced Charting the Unknown, a DLC which adds even more elements to a game already buckling under the weight of mechanical overload. Now you can build ships and sail them around Pywel's azure-blue waters. You can dive below the waves and swim with the fishes. You can also fall in love, get married, and - should things not pan out - get divorced. The everything game has become, well, even more everything in quintessentially audacious style. On that note, just wait until you see some of its supermassive Evangelion-esque maritime bosses.

Though don't get too excited. Like the base game, the DLC is a mixed bag. First, shipfaring lacks any kind of drama. Islands are so spread out as to make the actual journey painfully boring: impressionistic oceanic voyaging à la Wind Waker this is not. The diving, though, is beautifully executed. Underwater locales are notably serene, free from many of the irritating distractions that plague ground traversal.

As for the new narrative threads involving all three playable characters - grizzled Kliff, Bayonetta-alike Damiane, and gentle giant orc Oongka - they're equally uneven. Kliff's is the most interesting, transplanting the player to a desert cloaked in a thick orange sandstorm that, without the aid of a working compass, must be mapped out using beacons resembling luminescent dongs. It's pleasingly disorientating, a rare example of Pearl Abyss pushing a kind of intentional design friction, rather than accidentally creating it by inadequately explaining how to interact with the game.

Alas, there are also bugs, one of which was game-halting: Damiane's steed wouldn't spawn when I needed to follow Kliff on horseback. It was a reminder that for all the visual splendour, Crimson Desert can often feel like it's being held together by the code equivalent of gaffer tape.

Everything controls about as well as you'd expect if you've played the base game, i.e. not very well at all. Kliff may be able to run on water and soar through the sky like a cross between Matrix star Neo and Jesus, but he remains a bastard to control for even the most basic tasks. Why can't I lock onto the object a foot away? Why is he doing a three-hit combo somersault through the air when all I tried to do is pull out his lantern? These issues are compounded by the new romance and socialising options that appear every time you target an NPC: Crimson Desert becomes even fiddlier as it introduces these additional layers.

As the hours pass by - as I run, gallop, and bound through the arresting landscape in a manner that is comparatively frictionless compared to the rest of the game - my mind begins to numb. For all the extraneous mechanical gubbins, missions remain brainlessly simple: cavort from one waypoint to another and do a few simple button presses, or crack a few skulls to chug the narrative along. But then perhaps a certain numbing is the point of Crimson Desert? At a time when the real world is markedly inhospitable (diminishing job prospects, the ongoing cost-of-living crisis, the looming threat of geopolitical meltdown), Pearl Abyss' epic swaddles the player like a swords-and-sorcery comfort blanket. It is a safe space to disappear into for hours. Leave your worldly woes at the start screen.

The impression this mash-up of a game appears to want to impart is generosity: here, Pearl Abyss seems to say, is a product capable of accommodating your every wish, will, and whim. It appears to have left no gameplay stone unturned in its ultimate quest to provide the player with total satisfaction - a kind of blissed-out contentment at the thought of never having to leave this almost divine realm.

But rather than generous, perhaps there is something nefarious about Crimson Desert's maximalist design philosophy. It strikes me again that this is the most uniquely modern title I've ever played, as if the Grand Theft Auto and Assassin's Creed franchises, countless MMORPGs (the genre in which Pearl Abyss cut its teeth), and many hyper-compulsive mobile titles were squished together in an attention-devouring final form. One so mightily captivating, so singularly consuming, that the player keeps playing until their lifeforce is depleted and they can play no more.

That seems to be the way these "forever" games curve: toward the player's surrender - of mind, body, and time. And at least until Grand Theft Auto 6 is released in a few weeks, Crimson Desert and its new DLC seem to be the purest expression yet of this type of thing. Wading through its sheer mass of hit-or-miss distractions (which could expand even more), the design ethos begins to grate. As if I'm stuck in an elaborate, endlessly bifurcating maze of hamster wheels, always busy, though never enjoying any discernible pay-off. And so, as its presentational lustre wears off further, and its eye-popping massiveness becomes less of a novelty, Crimson Desert begins to hit differently: much less high-fantasy heaven and more like hell.

#10577. What we've been playing - "Sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Well, hello! I am once again wearing the Bertie hat in your usual host's absence, so joyous welcomes to our regular feature in which we talk about what we've been playing in recent times. This week, Marie has been revisiting Zelda - although perhaps not the one you might expect - while Dom is rediscovering Vice City of the 80s. Kelsey is playing some very weird cards, Connor is back in WoW - possibly forever - and I'm equally smitten with a different RPG.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. Bye!

Once again, I am finally playing a game from my backlog that I should've played years ago, and that's the delightful albeit dark card game Inscryption. There's so much more to this than you'd first expect, and I'm surprised the community has been able to keep the game's best bits under wraps for so long. Ultimately, nothing could've prepared me for Act 2, and my only real qualm with the whole experience is that I wish Act 2 had its own endless roguelike mode; you'd never be able to pull me away from it.

The game falters a little at the start of Act 3, I'll admit, and I've found myself very frustrated by what is an otherwise relatively easy time. This is down to me missing some rather useful earlier cards that I've since learnt would've made this final stretch much breezier, but oh well. I will still take down the Uberbots one way or another for the love of the game!

I've sunk every free hour I have into World of Warcraft: Forever since its release on Wednesday night. As of writing I am Level 19, waiting for an escort quest to come up in Westfall. So far things seem largely the same as in the original MMO, though the little changes and improvements I've seen have firmly planted a smile on my face.

This includes the new weather system. Like many others I was caught in a snow storm in Dun Morogh this week, a seemingly random environmental transformation that drastically limits view distance and blankets your screen with the cold stuff. It adds a lot, I think, to the fantasy of the game. I'll be playing this all weekend and for the foreseeable future.

I'll let you guess why I'm playing this right now of all times. Ahead of Grand Theft Auto 6's launch, I've made it my unofficial job to internalise the map of Vice City. I'm spending time driving around the strip, familiarising myself with the docklands, going for a few rounds of golf up in Leaf Links, and sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island. I'm immensely curious how similar the map is in GTA 6, and can't wait to see what's changed in the interceding 40 years in-universe. It's even better having V-Rock blast Iron Maiden and Twisted Sister at me; over the past few evenings, I've been playing this extra loud so it drowns out the sound of local fireworks for the sake of my poor, terrified dog. I don't think he's a fan of Bruce 'the air raid siren' Dickinson's voice - but it's better than all the apocalyptic banging.

The cold winter nights are drawing in, which calls for blankets, a warm drink, and a nice peaceful game before bed. I don't have an exact reason for going back to Tears of the Kingdom. Perhaps it's me diving into Ocarina of Time that fuelled my curiosity about returning, but what's surprised me the most is there's still so much I didn't see or do in my first 190 hours with it.

I started again, cleared my save file, and it turns out I've completely forgotten most things about the game - which is by no means a complaint! I'm rather enjoying making the little discoveries that are either brand-new to me, or ones that make me go 'AH-HA, I remember you' (in the case of Gloom Hands, the reaction was 'NOPE, NOPE, NOPE, NOPE.')

I've also had another surprise. I play in handheld mode mostly, and ToTK was all looking a bit blurry on the Switch 2's screen. So I got the upgrade pack and suddenly everything became beautiful again - Link was in focus, the individual blades of grass in Hyrule Field were clearly whipping in the wind, and the crispy snow around Rito village almost sparkled. I don't often get upgrade packs for games I already own, but this one was worth it.

I suspect this won't be the last time you see me yapping about Queen's Domain here. I've already enthused about it elsewhere on the site this week, but I've managed to squeeze in a few more hours since then, and it continues to enthrall. Here's a small moment from my most recent go: I'd been fumbling about the Tower of Knowledge for a while at this point, dying slightly more than I'd like to those jabby gargoyle gits and lumbering chimera things, so I thought I'd have a little wander away from the violence and have an explore. That was when I noticed the gap on the bookshelf, wide enough to slot in the mysterious tome - the Ratty Bug Compendium - I'd been wondering about ever since I picked it up elsewhere some five hours earlier.

Moments later a secret passage had opened, leading out to a balcony overlooking the ocean. Treasure! Exciting! But just as I'm about to go back inside, I notice a little outcrop on the exterior of the tower, seemingly reachable with my sword's magic dashing power. So over I go, discovering an otherwise inaccessible route into an inner chamber. There, I find a lever, which I excitedly pull, only to be rewarded with... nothing at all. Until five minutes or so later, when I discover a secret panel has now opened up on the other side of the tower, granting me access to the treats within. In isolation, it's not a remarkable gaming anecdote, but Queen's Domain is just crammed with these little incremental secrets and surprises, full of clever design that makes exploration feel so rewarding. I've already spotted more enigmas waiting to be cracked, and if Queen's Domain keeps distracting me like this, I might never escape its pull.

#10575. World’s first superconducting quantum heat engine could help unlock massive quantum computers

A newly developed superconducting quantum heat engine could deepen our understanding of thermodynamics while helping advance technologies needed for quantum computers with very large numbers of qubits.

Scientists are getting a clearer picture of how thermodynamics behaves in the quantum world, and that progress could benefit both quantum technology and our understanding of familiar thermodynamic principles. Researchers at Aalto University have now taken an important step by demonstrating the first cyclic quantum heat engine built inside a superconducting circuit.

The experiment connects two areas of physics that normally describe very different scales. Quantum mechanics explains the behavior of matter at extremely small scales, even below the size of atoms, while thermodynamics describes how heat and energy behave in much larger systems, from collections of molecules to the universe itself. Bringing the two together raises a fundamental question: what happens to familiar thermodynamic processes when quantum effects such as tunneling, entanglement, and superposition enter the picture?

A Heat Engine Built for the Quantum World

Conventional heat engines turn heat into useful work. James Watt's steam engine is one famous example, but the same basic concept remains central to modern transportation and electricity production, powering cars, ships, planes and many power plants.

The researchers have now created the world's first superconducting quantum heat engine. The extremely small device combines a transmon qubit, a resonator and a quantum refrigerator.

Operating under ultracold quantum conditions, the engine was able to use the tiny amount of available heat to repeatedly produce positive work. Achieving this kind of cyclic operation has been an important objective for researchers working on quantum heat engines. The result provides a proof of concept for superconducting heat engines that could eventually contribute to improved quantum computing technology.

The study, led by Academy Professor Mikko Möttönen, was published in Nature Communications.

Recreating an Otto Cycle Near Absolute Zero

To make the engine operate, the researchers reproduced an Otto cycle inside a superconducting circuit. The Otto cycle is a thermodynamic process also used in car engines and other conventional machines.

"In our experiment, we built a nanofabricated heat engine using superconducting circuits and operated it in a cryostat near absolute zero. At its heart is a transmon qubit, one of the basic building blocks of modern quantum technologies," says Tuomas Uusnäkki, the study's first author.

The researchers connected the transmon qubit to a quantum circuit refrigerator, allowing them to control heat flow on the quantum scale and demonstrate that this heat could be transformed into measurable work. A conventional heat engine normally relies on separate hot and cold environments. In this system, however, the same quantum refrigerator can supply both heating and cooling.

"Our quantum-circuit refrigerator can be tuned to both heat and cool the qubit on demand. Using carefully timed control pulses, we drove the engine in an Otto cycle and monitored the qubit state as the engine ran," explains Uusnäkki.

Measurements showed that heat passing through the qubit during the cycle was producing positive work.

"This is the first experimental demonstration of a cyclic quantum heat engine in superconducting circuits. Using a single controllable quantum refrigerator as both the hot and cold environment of the engine makes it simpler and more versatile," says Uusnäkki.

Toward Autonomous Quantum Computer Hardware

The researchers are now trying to improve the design and eventually develop a fully autonomous heat engine. One possible use would be reading out qubits without having to carry a microwave pulse from millikelvin temperatures all the way to room temperature.

That capability could become especially valuable as quantum computers grow. Autonomous devices integrated directly into superconducting circuits could reduce both the cost and complexity of machines containing very large numbers of qubits.

"Finland's Quantum Technology Strategy envisions a quantum computer with one thousand logical qubits by 2035, which probably means hundreds of thousands of physical qubits. Doing that with current technology requires millions of microwave cables costing thousand euros each. The cables also introduce noise into the system. Using autonomous devices instead would mostly eliminate the need for those cables," Möttönen says.

Reducing the need for those microwave connections could therefore address two challenges at once: the enormous hardware requirements of large quantum computers and the unwanted noise that cables can introduce into quantum systems.

The pioneering experiment was carried out using OtaNano, Finland's national research infrastructure for nano, micro and quantum technology. Funding came from the Research Council of Finland and the Finnish Cultural Foundation.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10574. Viral Cowboy who took his horse on bus responds after Detroit launches investigation

A horse owner has responded after a viral video showed them taking their horse onto a bus, and even the city is investigating.

When you’re using public transport, it isn’t a shock to see a dog sitting next to its owner, or other pets being transported in carry cases. These are pretty common sights for regular bus and train riders.

However, on November 9, the internet’s attention was caught by a viral video of two horses being led onto a bus in Detroit. In the clip, which was filmed from across the street, the two riders approached the door while holding their horses on a leash.

Both horses and riders were able to get on board the bus briefly, but they didn’t stick around for long. That left the woman recording the scene, Elessia Drew, in disbelief. “This is crazy,” she said.

The video has been reposted across social media since Drew first posted it to Facebook, attracting millions of views.

Jordan Williams, the horse’s owner, told WXYZ that he helped his horse on board for a funny video, but has taken them on buses before. “We walked on, and I must tell you the buses are a lot smaller than they used to be, or I may have been a little bigger,” he said.

“The point is just to bring light to… like there are cowboys that look like us.”

However, the Detroit Department of Transport has said they are taking the “taking appropriate steps to address this incident” as it is “not consistent with DDOT policy and potentially creates an unsafe and uncomfortable ride for our passengers.”

Williams told WXYZ that he hopes they “look at it like a joke like everyone else,” rather than taking any action.

#10572. Despite promising a heady mix of Vampire Survivors and Warhammer fan-service, Warhammer Survivors is somehow less than the sum of its parts

You know my favourite thing about Vampire Survivors? The slow build of strength, the swelling power fantasy, the edging. Developer Poncle has proved itself incredibly adept at goading players into ever more ludicrous setups, demonstrating that with some patience, a canny eye for build-crafting, and some very rudimentary knowledge of your surroundings, even an old man armed with garlic can be an extinction level event for the amassing undead. Warhammer Survivors sadly never reaches those heights.

Created by Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 studio Auroch Digital, Warhammer Survivors is - as its name suggests - an officially-licensed attempt to transplant developer Poncle's winning Vampire Survivors' formula to the Warhammer universe. Cue 40+ characters ripped from Warhammer lore getting the action-roguelike treatment, crossbows instead of clock lancets, rocket launchers instead of fire wands, bomb Squids instead of skeletons… you get the idea.

But this fusion doesn't really work. While Vampire Survivors was always a bit of an uphill battle from the start, it still hooked you with a thread that pulled you forward, tempting you with power-ups and other intriguing toys to play with en route. If hour one was a slog, hour five sparked curiosity, and hour 10 was a romp. In Warhammer Survivors, however, hour 10 feels like just as much of a slog as hour one. And even though Warhammer Survivors offers two alternate universes - the grimdark future of 40K and the old-school fantasy of Age of Sigmar, each a full game in its own right - this does little to alleviate the grind.

It's all so slow. Characters move slowly, upgrades come slowly, unlocking the mode that makes things move a bit faster comes slowly. Every inch feels like a mile, and the moreish power creep - tantalisingly propulsive in Vampire Survivors - is out of reach for so long per run here, it's discouraging. That's not to say there isn't any resolution to all the tension. Every now and then, you'll roll the right dice, appease the right gods, make the right moves. Your miniature pixel heroes will overcome the odds, and burn through the writhing mass of Orks, Skaven, Tyranids, Nurgle, or whatever other bastards are trying to get you down today.

The Survivors genre - sometimes known as 'bullet heaven' now - lives and dies on the strength of the over-powered nonsense that emerges as all your weapons and evolutions start to harmonise with each other in brutal synchronicity. Warhammer Survivors seems reluctant to let you get to this level, though. Even 30 hours in (!), some runs will end before they ever really get started - no matter how many skips, rerolls, or other chance-altering upgrades you've committed to your save file.

And because this is really two games glued into one, you have to do all the requisite grinding and levelling on both sides. With massive maps and impossibly slow character movement (especially on some of the bulkier characters), collecting all the items per location is more of a chore than it is fun. This wouldn't be so bad if these items were just curios, but in most cases they're pretty significant quality-of-life enhancers. Why are you hiding them from me, Auroch!?

It's almost impressive how Warhammer Survivors manages to feel like a step back from Poncle's original in many ways: yes, there's more content, but there appears to be a lack of understanding of why this genre immediately took off and spawned a myriad of imitators. The very worst weapons are the only ones available at the beginning, so unlocking new characters and armaments is necessary rather than a fun bonus. Lore is paper thin - in Vampire Survivors, we had a blank canvas and the lack of lore didn't really matter, but Warhammer is steeped in its own mythos, so relegating it to merely set dressing feels remiss. Some of the synergies between items and their evolutions don't really make sense. The maps are awkward and fussy to navigate. The locked requirements to take on stage-end bosses are even more cryptic than in Vampire Survivors. In almost all the ways that matter when it comes to fun, Warhammer Survivors pales in comparison to its forebear. This wouldn't be so egregious if the game wasn't trying to emulate it quite so completely, but it is - and as such, all the actually decent design choices hidden behind layers of frustration are marred.

As a Warhammer and Survivors fan, I've found myself floating back to the game, even if - sometimes - my eyes roll back into my head within minutes of my first run. But it's not a complete disaster: on those occasions where the stars do align, Warhammer Survivors finds its way to a power fantasy worthy of both franchises. You'll grab a Flamer early doors, then pick up the passive Glue to reduce incoming damage. You'll gain the Heavy Flamer, the Boltgun, and the projectile-multiplying Dice Cube, eventually unlocking the Heavy Bolter - slicing through enemies and all but melting the face off that pesky Prophet of Gork.

I just wish there wasn't so much friction in actually getting to that point. I imagine Warhammer Survivors is the sort of game I'll keep on my harddrive (and on multiple devices; it costs a fiver and it's on Game Pass) for a while. If you can endure the teething pains and engage with the wobblier systems, there's the usual Survivors payoff waiting for you as the screen fills with pixelated blood and countless helpless enemies fall to your blades. Maybe the stress of the early game even makes this catharsis even better. So even with its qualms, Warhammer Survivors is still an enjoyable, lo-fi stress reliever to snack on in between other, meatier games.

Android, Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S, iOS

#10570. World of Warcraft: Forever's magical opening week reminds us all about the power of the MMO, and the threat it poses to your free time

World of Warcraft: Forever is a time vampire. Since its release last Wednesday, I have spent 78 hours in Azeroth. Some of that I was AFK, sure. But for many of those hours, I was lost, happily, in the rolling hills and vast planes of Blizzard's MMO - now enhanced, beautified, and given a new social energy that makes the old feel wonderfully new.

The original World of Warcraft is so beloved, so influential, that I wasn't convinced Forever could build upon it without cheapening the overall experience; without creating glaring clashes between its new additions and legacy elements that, in some instances, are now over two decades old. And while there's still plenty I haven't seen after 78 hours and 35 levels, I can speak to one truth: WoW Forever is stunning work.

Perhaps it's best to start with WoW: Forever's most obvious changes: its new lighting and weather effects. People love to speak about getting lost in MMOs, being consumed by their digital worlds, but as much as I've enjoyed World of Warcraft in the past, its ageing graphics tech never managed to ensnare me that way. Forever's new visual elements, though, have gone a long way toward pulling me in.

As I look out to sea, waves roll across the ocean. Inland, water flows fast along the rivers of Stranglethorn Vale. These are largely cosmetic additions, sure, but they make Azeroth feel so much more real. Dun Morogh's snowstorms - likely the first exposure many players will have to Forever's new weather effects - transform a peaceful, picturesque landscape into something hostile at a moment's notice. It's the same for Tanris; its vast desert now blighted with sandstorms that bring raging life to an otherwise fairly barren zone.

World of Warcraft; Forever's enhancements go beyond visual, of course. Before launch, Blizzard described Forever as an opportunity to address imbalances, and to show a bit of love to underserved playstyles and classes. That's why I decided I'd play as a druid and work toward creating a balance caster: a ranged DPS druid far slower to level than a feral one, and far less useful as a magic DPS compared to a mage. In other words, an unoptimal choice for the truly silly.

I actually levelled a balance druid to max in Classic World of Warcraft some years back and it was an arduous process. In Forever, though, adjustments made to the spells and talents have made it much less frustrating so far. You do more damage, for starters, and alterations to the talent tree and unlockable skills mean you don't run into the sort of mana problems that crippled balance druids previously. From what I've seen, all the ugly duckling class specialisations - like Retribution Paladins and Enhancement Shamans - have received similar changes.

My interest had been similarly piqued before launch by the talk of WoW: Forever's new dungeons. I still need more time to understand how these additions affect the wider leveling process, but already I can see they've been crafted with care, fitting nicely in the gaps between the original dungeons on a progression level.

Hall of Thanes is a Ragefire Chasm equivalent for Alliance players, serving as a solid introduction to dungeons for newcomers before they head off to The Deadmines and beyond. Here, you delve into the bowels of Ironforge, navigating tombs and tunnels in search of treasure and tackling foes beneath the seat of dwarvish power. It's simple and clean like all early game Classic dungeons are, made with such faithfulness to the original vision that I could be convinced it was always present.

It's also a lovely narrative introduction to one of the world's many antagonists, the Dark Iron Dwarves. In the original World of Warcraft, players would first encounter them across a scattering of quests before being thrust towards Blackrock Mountain. Now, though, they show up early and immediately plant intrigue. What's the deal with these drilling machines? What about the fire elementals they're with?

Forever's new dungeons also introduce some well-crafted quests. Anyone who's played Classic WoW can recount the frustration of dungeons that, thanks to some awkward design, often require two separate completions to finish off. Blackfathom Deeps is a prime example; there are never enough twilight pendants to go around, forcing players to go back in for a second run if they want to tick it off. As such, many, myself included, don't bother. World of Warcraft: Forever, though - with some exceptions - appears to favour a one-and-done approach to dungeons. In the new Excavation Site: Wetlands, for instance, there are just enough raptors hidden in tall grass for everyone to complete their hides in one go. A little thing, but meaningful.

But beyond all this, perhaps the most wonderful thing about World of Warcraft: Forever is how full it feels. It's lively, bustling with action and excitement. Some of this is just that new MMO energy, but Blizzard's new mega server approach, which vastly increases the number of players you'll see doing quests compared to a regular server, is undoubtedly a significant factor too.

Every major city is packed with people having their own adventure, each quest zone is littered with heroes doing their bit to save the world. And this opens the door to social interactions, the beating heart of any MMO. The sight of dozens of players jumping off a zeppelin or flooding onto an elevator in unison is a joy, and grouping with strangers for an escort quest or an elite quest is a curative for digital hermits.

At one point, I find myself drawn into a conversation with a mage during a quest for troll ears. The mobs were hotly contested, and Horde players were quick to snatch tags. The few minutes I spent there, talking about what's going on and how we're finding the game were lovely, and World of Warcraft: Forever's set-up encourages so much more of this kind of thing.

So, while I probably have plenty more sleepless nights left to go before I max out my druid, World of Warcraft: Forever has made a strong early impression. It's taken the original game and expanded on it in genuinely meaningful ways, leaving the things that worked untouched while enriching the elements needed some love. It's a wonderful point to jump into the game if you're a new player, then, and a refreshed classic full of twists for the returning veteran. Whatever the coming months and years may bring, this opening week has been magical.

#10569. Scientists tracked kids for 8 years — the screen time result was unexpected

A Finnish study has found an unexpected association between screen use and cognitive performance. Children who accumulated more screen time as they grew up tended to show better cognitive processing during adolescence. One of the researchers cautions against viewing screen time as entirely harmful and says the goal should be to balance physical activity with screen use that encourages active thinking.

Low levels of physical activity among adolescents remain a significant public health concern. Sedentary behavior has been associated with poorer academic achievement, while physical activity is known to support brain function, particularly in adults. Much less is known, however, about how physical activity and sedentary behavior during childhood and adolescence relate to cognitive processing later in adolescence.

These years are especially important because the brain is still developing. Factors that shape cognitive abilities during childhood and adolescence may have lasting effects that extend into adulthood, potentially influencing education and working life.

Researchers examined how physical activity, sedentary behavior, and screen time from childhood through adolescence were related to cognitive processing in the teenage years. They also looked for differences between girls and boys and investigated whether the intensity of physical activity played a role.

More Screen Time Linked to Better Cognitive Processing

The results showed that greater screen time beginning in childhood was associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

"The findings suggest that screen time can support children's and adolescents' cognitive processing. Presumably, the essential point here is what kind of things they do in their screen time. Teachers and parents should encourage children to use devices and screens in such ways that promote active thinking, problem-solving, creativity and learning," states Doctoral Researcher Petri Jalanko from the University of Jyväskylä.

The findings suggest that the type of screen activity may be important. Digital activities that involve learning, creativity, problem-solving, or other forms of active mental engagement could potentially help explain the association observed in the study.

"We should not regard screen time solely as harmful but seek balance between physical activity and screen time that promotes active thinking," Jalanko summarizes.

Physical Activity Showed Different Links in Girls and Boys

The relationship between physical activity and cognition was more complicated.

Among girls, greater amounts of light intensity physical activity since childhood were associated with better working memory during adolescence. Among boys, greater participation in guided physical activity from childhood through adolescence was linked to better working memory.

The researchers also found an unexpected pattern involving unsupervised exercise. Lower levels of self-reported unsupervised physical activity were associated with better cognitive processing in adolescence.

By contrast, physical activity and sedentary time measured using a device that tracked heart rate and movement were not associated with cognitive processing. One possible explanation is that these sensors can measure movement and physiological activity but cannot determine exactly what a person is doing during periods of activity or inactivity.

"Our study indicates that the connections of physical activity and sedentary behavior to cognitive processing are complex and depend on the sex, the type and assessment method of physical activity and sedentary time. Moreover, boys and girls may benefit from different types of physical activity in view of brain health," Jalanko adds.

More Research Is Needed to Determine Cause and Effect

The researchers emphasize that the findings show associations rather than proving that screen time or particular types of physical activity directly cause changes in cognitive performance.

"However, we need more intervention studies to find out causal relations and sex differences in this respect. Moreover, it is important to examine more specifically the effects of the intensity of physical activity on changes taking place in cognitive processing."

The results come from an eight-year follow-up of the PANIC study on children's physical activity and nutrition. The current analysis included 124 girls and 136 boys with an average age of 15.8 years.

Researchers measured physical activity and sedentary behavior using both survey questionnaires and a device that combined heart rate and movement measurements. Learning, attention, and working memory were evaluated using the CogState test battery.

The research was published in the journal Pediatric Exercise Science.

#10566. The European Commission's new Digital Fairness Act could change monetisation in games forever - and not just in Europe

"Well, we'll just do this for the rest of the world."

The European Commission has published its proposed Digital Fairness Act, which has the potential to change how video games are monetised - worldwide - forever. A draft of the Act was leaked yesterday and what's been published today appears to be, to my eye, the same, though there's now also a mini-site accompanying it that explores the Act in more detail and uses more understandable language.

And note that while the Digital Fairness Act isn't about video games exclusively - it's much broader than that - it does mention video games specifically, highlighting both their increased importance to society now, and that policy-shaping bodies like the European Commission are prepared to take them seriously.

The Digital Fairness Act is set to become European law, probably sometime between 2028 and 2029, once everyone's discussed it and made whatever amendments they're going to make to it. And at that point, breaching the regulations will come with potentially steep fines (up to six percent of a company's worldwide annual turnover, or up to €2m where an annual turnover isn't available).

The biggest things in the Digital Fairness Act relating to video games are regulations around loot boxes (sometimes referred to as "variable reward systems" or systems with "uncertainty-based rewards") and virtual currencies, particularly with regard to "minors" - anyone under 18 years old.

The Act stipulates paid-for virtual currencies must be disabled in a game by default for minors, then activated by parental and guardian approval, and that paid loot boxes "shall not" be offered to minors at all. Moreover, games must provide a way for everyone to disable paid loot boxes completely, and clearly show the probability of receiving items from them.

It's these changes that have the potential to overhaul how loot boxes work everywhere going forwards, and whether companies use them at all. To understand why, I talked to games business expert George Osborn, who runs the excellent Video Games Industry Memo newsletter, and who's been following the Act closely.

Osborn sees this as a "really big" regulatory change and believes that companies like EA, Xbox, Activision Blizzard, PlayStation, and other big gaming companies, both in console gaming and mobile gaming, will be "really concerned" by this. That's partly because a lot of these things being outlined are not necessarily easy to implement.

For starters, a company now needs a way to work out who's a minor playing its game and who isn't, and it needs to provide "a master switch", as Osborn calls it, for people to switch paid loot boxes off with. These things require not just design changes but perhaps entire alterations to a game's approach; it means theoretically being able to turn paid card packs off in EA Sports FC Ultimate Team, example, and their not being able to be sold to minors at all.

"EA might be looking at it and going, 'This isn't great news for [EA Sports FC] Ultimate Team because people can switch it off,'" Osborn added, "but Ultimate Team is at least hived off in a box." EA could potentially age-gate Ultimate team as a mode and deal with it that way. But what if you're a card game where random paid rewards are central to your premise? "What that now means is you're possibly going to have to find a way to just completely turn that off for users, and it is very difficult to do that when they're embedded into the design of the game."

The virtual currency issue is potentially easier to tackle because existing parental controls can control spending, so those tools could expand to cover it. But another proposal brought forward in the Digital Fairness Act states that a game must clearly show, in a region's local currency, how much an item costs when bought with virtual currency, which adds another layer of complication when considering different currencies and people moving between countries with their devices.

These changes require investment and thought, and they sit alongside broader changes that affect things like subscription services, such as Xbox Live and PlayStation Plus and Nintendo Online, to make them easier to cancel and less able to obfuscate prices and hidden extras. There's even a stipulation that people be able to talk to a human in customer service rather than a chat bot. There are more technicalities for game-making companies to consider.

But why should people outside of the European Union care? These rules are only enforceable there. Because: if a company is selling a game into the European Union, which I expect every game company will want to do - "you don't just want to rule out a market of a few hundred million consumers; it's not a light thing to cut that out", Osborn says - then it will have to comply by these rules. And if it does, how many different versions of a game will it be prepared to make?

Think about the recent global move towards all devices using USB-C chargers: that was a movement started by a European Commission ruling. Think of the new Switch 2s with removable battery packs: that was another change instigated by the European Commission. "Because that market is big enough, you design for it," Obsorn says, "and then your logic becomes, 'Well, we'll just do this for the rest of the world.' You're not going to produce one with the battery sealed in and the battery without. You're just going to find one way to produce things.

"That's likely to be the biggest impact in the UK and the rest of the world," he adds, "as people adjust the designs of their games to follow these rules. There's going to be a strong likelihood that some of the design features will remain within the games because it's easiest to work off a single universal version."

The other thing, which Osborn thinks is more important, is that policy makers around the world are "desperate" for solutions to problems and don't know where to look for them. "So if someone comes up with an answer to a question that's been annoying everybody, like 'what do we do about loot boxes?'," he says, "and it provides a particularly strong - or perceived to be effective - solution, it'll be copied. And it'll be copied because a big regulatory bloc like the European Union, which is known for protecting consumers particularly well, has decided this is a good thing. People trust it."

Then, because companies have already developed a way to comply with those rules, it becomes difficult for them to argue that they can't do it elsewhere. "Because those regulators go, 'Well why not?'" Osborn says. "Because if you build the solution in one place, you can build it elsewhere."

Whether or not this will be the end of loot boxes as we know them, Osborn isn't sure. "My guess would be this will continue to push loot boxes into the fringes of video game monetisation. It was already starting to happen," he says. "I know some really high-profile games have them very prominently, which makes people feel like is there really any change here? But I know a lot of companies behind the scenes started to quietly move away from them in the last few years when they were a reputational problem. If you move to a world in which you're meant to have a massive off-switch in your game for them, that's a world in which fewer companies will go in that direction."

Some will stick with it. "If you're an EA, it's going to be very difficult to move away from the model that has supported your business for years," Osborn says. And card-based deck-building games will struggle to avoid it. "But overall, we're going to see a shift away from it," he adds. "And more broadly what this marks is a much wider crackdown on the way that video games monetise their players. As you start to see that crackdown come into force, game developers are going to have to think much more carefully about how the design decisions they make don't just interact with ethical practices, but actually with legal practices too."

#10564. How to Test AI-Generated Content Before It Reaches Your Users

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams now ship something an AI helped write: help-center articles, product descriptions, release notes, even code comments. The draft appears in seconds, and that speed is the trap. Nobody tests it the way they would test a new feature, so mistakes slip through that a quick review would have caught.

This guide is about the other side of AI testing. Instead of using AI to test your software, you test what AI hands you. Here is a simple routine that fits into a normal publishing or release process.

AI writing sounds confident whether or not it is right. A model can invent a statistic (researchers call this hallucination and have documented it across many text-generation systems), quote a feature your product does not have, or describe a setting that was renamed two releases ago. The sentence reads smoothly, so the error hides in plain sight.

Traditional QA does not cover this. A unit test tells you the page loads. It says nothing about whether the claim on the page is true. That gap is where most AI-related incidents begin.

1. Define what "good" means before you generate anything. Write down the audience, the claims you are willing to make and the sources you trust. A short brief makes the output easier to judge, because you are comparing it with something concrete.

2. Check every factual claim. Highlight each number, name and statement of fact, then verify it against a primary source. Google's own guidance on generative AI content tells publishers to fact-check AI output, including titles, descriptions and alt text, before publishing. If you cannot confirm a claim in a minute or two, cut it or rewrite it as something you can support.

3. Test the instructions. For tutorials and how-to content, follow the steps exactly as written on a clean machine or account. Commands that fail and menus that no longer exist show up immediately this way.

4. Review the voice. Replace vague phrases with specifics from your own experience: a real customer question, a real error message, a real number from your analytics. This is also what separates useful content from filler.

5. Keep a human owner. Every piece should have a named person who approved it. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework makes the same point at organization level: people stay accountable for how AI is used and evaluated. If something goes wrong later, you know who to ask and where to fix it.

Some teams also run drafts through an AI detector. That can be useful as a prompt for a closer look, for example when freelancers or vendors deliver work and you want to know how much of it was machine-generated. A high score is a reason to read more carefully. It is not proof of anything, and it should never decide an outcome on its own, because detectors make mistakes in both directions. A Stanford study found that GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers, and short texts are easy to misjudge too. If you want a quick second opinion on a draft, a tool like Tester AI can give you a starting point for that conversation.

Review works best when it is not optional. A few small habits make it stick.

Treating the first draft as finished. It is a starting point. Plan time for review the same way you plan time for writing.

Reviewing for grammar only. Clean sentences are not evidence of correct content. Check meaning first and polish second.

Letting the tool grade its own work. Asking the same model to confirm its answer rarely catches real errors. Use a source or a person.

Skipping legal and brand review. Generated text can echo existing material or make promises your company cannot keep. Anything customer-facing deserves a second pair of eyes.

AI can save hours of drafting, but speed only helps if the result is trustworthy. Treat generated content like code from a new contributor: useful, often good, and always reviewed before it goes live. A short routine of defining quality, verifying facts, testing instructions and keeping a human owner will catch most problems before your users do.

Do I need to test AI-generated content if a human edits it? Yes. Editing for style does not confirm that the facts are correct. Verify claims separately from polishing the wording.

How long should a review take? For a typical article, expect roughly the time it takes to read it twice plus a few minutes per factual claim. Reviews are faster when the brief and sources are prepared in advance.

Can an AI detector prove that text was written by a machine? No. Detectors estimate likelihood, and they can be wrong. Use the result as a signal for human review, never as the final decision.

What should I check first? Start with numbers, names, dates and anything a customer could act on, such as prices, steps or legal statements. Those carry the highest risk if they are wrong.

#10563. Tiny robots powered by light can hunt down and collect bacteria

Robots small enough to operate in the microbial world could give scientists a new way to directly handle objects that are impossible to manipulate by hand. These tiny machines are around 50 times smaller than the diameter of a human hair, bringing researchers closer to the long-pursued goal of interacting directly with the microscopic world.

That capability could be especially useful for biological materials in water, including individual cells and bacteria. Precisely controlling and moving objects at this scale has been a persistent challenge. The newly developed nanorobots show that it is already possible to collect bacteria, transport them, and release them at selected locations.

One of the biggest obstacles in developing machines this small is finding an effective way to propel and control them. At Julius-Maximilians-Universität Würzburg (JMU), a research team led by Professor Bert Hecht has been developing a solution that uses the recoil produced by individual photons to move microscopic devices known as microdrones.

The devices contain as many as four plasmonic nanoantennas. These antennas absorb light with a particular color and helicity, then emit that light in a specific direction. Redirecting each photon creates a tiny recoil force, similar in principle to the recoil produced when a bullet is fired. Because the microdrones have so little mass, those extremely small forces can generate substantial acceleration and speed.

For the latest work, the researchers reduced the size of their light-powered robots even further, producing devices smaller than one micrometer. Simplifying the steering system was an important part of achieving that size while retaining propulsion based on photon recoil.

The new control method takes advantage of nanoscale antenna wires built into the robot. These wires naturally tend to align with the polarization direction of incoming light. By changing the light's polarization, the researchers can control which direction the nanorobot faces. At the same time, photon recoil continues to propel it forward, creating a steering system that works somewhat like the directional control used in larger vehicles.

"In essence, we have built a light-driven nanorobot that can track down and collect bacteria," says Jin Qin, lead experimental scientist of the study. "By simplifying the design, we reached a size at which these robots can operate directly in the microbial world - almost like microscopic cleaning devices."

The robots are also highly maneuverable. They can make extremely fast 90° turns, helping them scan broad areas of a sample in an organized and efficient way. They can also selectively capture, carry, and release substantial numbers of bacteria.

Under controlled laboratory conditions, this means the nanorobots can effectively "clean" microscopic environments. They gather bacteria from one area and deposit them at specifically chosen locations.

"This is a striking example of how light can be used not only to observe the microscopic world, but also to actively shape it," adds Bert Hecht. "The idea of tiny robotic cleaners may sound futuristic, but we are already demonstrating the physical principles that make it possible."

The robots remain fully maneuverable even while carrying larger groups of bacteria, although their speed decreases somewhat under the added load. That ability to keep functioning while transporting larger bacterial clusters points to possible future uses in microbiology, biomedical research, and precise manipulation of materials at the microscopic scale.

#10559. Scientists turn DNA into a memory device that uses 100x less power

DNA serves as the genetic blueprint for every living organism, but it is also an extraordinarily dense way to store information. A single gram can hold about 215 million gigabytes of data. Bringing that remarkable storage capacity into electronics could lead to more efficient data centers, faster processing and systems capable of handling increasingly complex information.

The challenge has been finding a way to make biological DNA function effectively alongside electronic materials. Penn State researchers have now developed an approach designed to overcome that incompatibility.

The work, published in Advanced Functional Materials and the subject of a patent application, relies on two key components. One is synthetic DNA, made from commercially available, chemically engineered molecules arranged into short genetic sequences tailored for specific electronic requirements. The other is crystalline perovskite, a semiconductor already used in technologies including solar cells, lasers and data storage devices.

"Biology and electronics are different domains," said Kavya S. Keremane, co-corresponding author and postdoctoral researcher in materials science and engineering at Penn State. "Bridging these two fields required developing an entirely new materials platform that allows them to function seamlessly together. By combining the information storage capabilities of DNA with the exceptional electronic properties of perovskite semiconductors, we created a bio-hybrid system that fundamentally changes how low-power memory devices can be designed."

Using these materials, the team created a memory resistor, known as a "memristor," that operates with very little energy. Unlike ordinary resistors, which maintain a set resistance to electrical current in devices ranging from cell phones to space shuttles and lose their stored information when power disappears, memristors can preserve a record of previous electrical activity. They can remember the direction in which current previously flowed even after the power source is removed.

That ability allows information to be stored and processed in the same place, resembling the way neurons function in the brain. Such an arrangement could support more simultaneous and sophisticated forms of data processing. According to the researchers, however, practical commercial systems would still require enough storage capacity and electrical power to become costly and inefficient without DNA's ability to pack enormous amounts of information into a very small space while consuming little energy.

"As the demand for artificial intelligence (AI) grows, we need a new strategy for low-power, high-storage devices," said Bed Poudel, co-corresponding author and research professor of materials science and engineering at Penn State.

Poudel said AI and other emerging technologies are expected to depend increasingly on neuromorphic computing, which is designed to operate more like the human brain. Such systems can evaluate multiple inputs at once while making decisions informed by previous experiences and future priorities.

"Usually, it takes more power to store more information. Our device, however, consumes 100 times less power and the storage capacity is higher than traditional storage devices, like flash drives."

To construct the device, the researchers added silver nanoparticles to a layer of customized DNA sequences -- specially designed to be of certain compositions and lengths -- that was integrated with thin films of perovskite.

This technique, called "doping," involves introducing a small amount of another material to produce specific properties. In this case, adding the silver nanoparticles allowed the DNA to conduct electricity while also helping its molecular units line up in a more orderly arrangement.

Synthetic DNA offered another important advantage over natural DNA. Unlike natural DNA -- long, entangled strands that behave like wet spaghetti when handled -- short and rigid pieces of synthetic DNA can be arranged with much greater precision at extremely small scales.

According to co-author Neela H. Yennawar, research professor and director of the Penn State Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences' Biomolecular Interactions Core Facility, molecularly engineered DNA can provide structural organization, adjustable electrical conductivity and functional control that natural DNA cannot achieve when incorporated into thin films.

"We can computationally determine exactly which sequences we need and how long they should be, and then we can rationally design them with synthetic DNA," Yennawar said. "These structures can be systematically doped with silver and other ions and engineered to interface seamlessly with perovskites -- transforming DNA from a biological macromolecule into a programmable, multifunctional nanomaterials platform."

When combined, the silver-doped DNA and perovskite formed bio-hybrid pathways that directed the flow of electrical current through the device.

The researchers found that electrons moved reliably when they applied less than 0.1 volt -- for comparison, standard U.S. outlets have 120 volts -- and the device responded predictably when the direction of the current was changed.

The carefully designed DNA structures, combined with the perovskite, also helped make the device unusually stable. According to the team, it continued operating consistently at temperatures approaching 250 degrees Fahrenheit and remained functional at room temperature for more than six weeks, substantially exceeding the performance standards of existing perovskite-based memory storage devices.

The researchers also reported that the new system could perform the same memory function as comparable technologies while consuming only one-tenth as much power. That level of efficiency could make the approach especially attractive for future electronics designed to handle large amounts of information with lower energy demands.

"Using just the DNA or just perovskite alone did not produce near as robust a result as the combination," Keremane said. "It's this combination that enables a very high memory storage density that requires very little power."

A New Direction for Bio-Inspired Electronics

The team now plans to improve the technology further and explore additional uses for bio-inspired electronic systems.

"Nature has the solution -- we just have to find it and apply it," Poudel said. "This work of integrating DNA into electronics to do amazing things gives a glimpse into what is possible."

In addition to Keremane, Yennawar and Poudel, other Penn State co-authors include co-corresponding author Luyao Zheng, postdoctoral research in materials science and engineering; Haodong Wu, doctoral student in materials science and engineering; Jiamao Zheng, who was a master's student in materials science and engineering at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State; Shashank Priya, who was a professor of materials science and engineering at the time of research; and Chiranth C. Ravi, who was a master's student in the Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State. Abhinav Gorthy and co-corresponding author Rashmi Jha, chemical engineering and materials science, University of Minnesota, also contributed.

The U.S. National Science Foundation, the National Institutes of Health, Penn State and the University of Minnesota supported this research.
