#10556. The Hidden Problems Behind Website-to-CRM Integrations (and How to Prevent Them)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Connecting a website form to a customer relationship management (CRM) system may seem straightforward. A developer collects information from a form, sends it to an API endpoint, and checks whether a new contact appears in the CRM.

The integration may work perfectly during the first test. However, problems can appear after the website goes live. Some leads may not reach the CRM, duplicate contacts may appear, or important customer information may be missing.

These issues are not always easy to notice. A visitor might see a confirmation message even when the CRM has failed to save their information.

This guide explains common problems in website-to-CRM integrations and practical ways developers can prevent them.

A website-to-CRM integration allows information collected through a website to move into a CRM system. This usually happens through an API or webhook.

For example, when someone completes a contact form, the website collects their name, email address, phone number, and other details. The integration then sends this information to the CRM, where it can be stored and used by the relevant team.

An API integration provides a structured way for two applications to exchange information. Developers who need additional background can also read OpenHerald's overview of API integration.

The process usually involves several steps:

The website validates the submitted information.

The application sends a request to the CRM.

The CRM checks the request and processes the data.

The website or backend handles the response.

Each step needs to work correctly. If one part fails, the information may not reach its destination.

One important rule is to avoid displaying a successful submission message before the application has safely accepted and stored the data.

Data issues are among the most frequent problems in website-to-CRM integrations. They may not appear during development because test submissions usually contain simple, predictable information.

Real visitors enter data in different formats and may submit the same form more than once.

A visitor might complete a contact form and later download a resource using the same email address. If the integration creates a new CRM record every time, the same person may appear several times.

To prevent this, developers should decide how existing contacts will be identified.

Email addresses are commonly used for matching. Before comparing them, the application should remove unnecessary spaces and convert the address to a consistent format.

These values should be treated consistently.

Where supported, an upsert operation can create a new contact or update an existing one instead of creating unnecessary duplicates.

Website forms and CRM systems do not always use the same field structure.

A website may collect a visitor's full name in one field, while the CRM expects separate first-name and last-name values. Other fields may have different names or requirements.

Developers should create a clear mapping between website fields and CRM fields before building the integration.

They should also check which fields are required. If the CRM requires information that the website does not collect, the request may fail.

Different systems may store information in different formats.

For example, one application might use United Kingdom, while another expects UK. Dates, phone numbers, and country values can also create problems.

A reliable integration should validate and standardise these values before sending them to the CRM.

A contact record is more useful when the sales or marketing team knows where the visitor came from.

However, integrations sometimes send only basic contact information and leave out details such as campaign source, landing page, and referral information.

Developers should identify which tracking fields are useful and include them in the integration where appropriate.

Even an integration that works correctly at launch can stop working later.

Authentication credentials may expire, permissions may change, or an API provider may limit the number of requests an application can make.

Three areas deserve particular attention.

Many APIs use access tokens or other credentials to verify requests. Some tokens expire and must be refreshed.

If an application does not handle token renewal correctly, requests may begin failing without an obvious problem on the website.

Developers should implement the authentication process recommended by the CRM provider and monitor failed requests.

An API provider may restrict the number of requests allowed within a particular period.

A marketing campaign can suddenly increase website traffic and generate more form submissions than usual. If the integration exceeds the provider's limit, some requests may be rejected.

The application should recognise rate-limit responses and retry requests according to the provider's instructions.

An integration should not receive more access than it needs.

For example, a form submission service may only need permission to create or update contacts. Giving it full administrative access increases unnecessary security risks.

Use the minimum permissions required for the integration to function.

External services can experience outages, network delays, and temporary errors. If the website sends a form submission directly to the CRM without storing it first, a failed request could mean a lost lead.

A safer approach is to separate receiving a submission from delivering it to the CRM.

Validate the form information on the server.

Store the submission safely in your own system.

Send it to the CRM through a background process.

Retry temporary failures after an appropriate delay.

Flag permanent failures for manual review.

This approach gives the application another opportunity to deliver information when the CRM becomes available again.

A request might reach the CRM successfully, but the response could be lost because of a network problem. The website may then retry the same request, creating a duplicate contact.

Idempotency helps prevent this situation. The application can use a stable identifier for each submission so that repeating the same operation does not create another record.

For example, a combination of the form ID and a normalised email address may help identify repeated submissions. The exact approach depends on the CRM's API capabilities.

This is an illustrative example. Functions such as crmUpsertContact() and scheduleRetry() need to be implemented according to the application and CRM being used.

The important point is that every submission should have a known status: successfully delivered, waiting for another attempt, or requiring manual attention.

A working integration should also protect the quality and security of the information it collects.

Public forms can receive automated submissions from bots. Without appropriate protection, these entries can fill the CRM with unwanted records.

Validation should happen on the server, even if the website already checks information in the browser.

Some forms collect permission to send marketing communications.

If a visitor selects a consent checkbox, that information should also be passed to the CRM. Otherwise, the CRM may contain the contact details without a reliable record of the visitor's choice.

Where relevant, store the consent value, the wording or version displayed, and the time the visitor gave consent.

An integration should only send the information needed for its intended purpose.

Collecting unnecessary personal details increases the amount of information the application must protect and maintain.

Developers should also understand where submitted information is stored, including databases, queues, application logs, and the CRM.

Testing an integration before launch is important, but monitoring it afterwards is just as necessary.

A successful test does not prove that every future submission will work.

The following checks can help identify problems early.

Compare the number of website form submissions with the number of contacts successfully created or updated in the CRM.

A growing difference between the two numbers may indicate that some requests are failing.

Application logs should record the reason a request failed and include a submission identifier where possible.

Avoid storing complete personal details in logs unless there is a specific, justified need.

Developers should configure notifications when the integration's failure rate exceeds an acceptable threshold.

This makes it easier to investigate problems before they affect a large number of submissions.

A scheduled test submission can help confirm that the complete process is still working.

For example, a test lead can be sent periodically and checked to confirm that it reaches the CRM correctly.

Someone should be responsible for monitoring the integration.

This person or team should review errors, check API changes, monitor credentials, and investigate changes to CRM fields.

Without a clear owner, small problems can remain unnoticed for a long time.

Documentation makes integrations easier to maintain and troubleshoot.

It does not need to be complicated. A short reference document covering the main rules can save considerable time when another developer needs to work on the system.

Before deployment, developers should also test unusual situations.

Testing these situations helps reveal weaknesses that ordinary form submissions may not expose.

What is the most common website-to-CRM integration problem?

Duplicate contacts and incorrect field mapping are common problems. They usually happen when the website and CRM handle data differently.

How can developers prevent duplicate contacts?

Normalise email addresses, use a consistent matching rule, and use an upsert operation or idempotency mechanism where the CRM supports it.

Should a website send form data directly to a CRM?

For more reliable handling, the application should first validate and safely store the submission. A background process can then deliver it to the CRM and retry temporary failures.

How can I tell if a CRM integration has stopped working?

Compare website submission counts with CRM records, monitor failed requests, and schedule regular test submissions.

Who should be responsible for maintaining the integration?

Assign a developer or technical team to monitor the connection, review errors, maintain credentials, and respond to API changes.

Developers can make these integrations more reliable by validating information on the server, storing submissions before delivery, using safe retry mechanisms, and monitoring the results after launch.

The goal is simple: every valid submission should reach the CRM, and every failed submission should be visible and recoverable rather than silently lost.

#10554. NASA just used satellites and debris to navigate without GPS

NASA's Starling mission has reached another milestone in autonomous spaceflight by demonstrating a system that can determine a satellite's orbital position using other objects in space as reference points rather than depending on an external navigation network.

The technology, called FALCON (Fast Autonomous Lost-in-space Catalog-based Optical Navigation), is designed to help spacecraft operate with greater independence. Such capabilities could become increasingly important as NASA pursues missions farther from Earth, including lunar satellite swarms, distributed science missions, and future human exploration.

Satellites near Earth commonly rely on GPS for navigation, but those signals may be weak, unreliable, or unavailable around the Moon and in deep space. FALCON offers an alternative by allowing spacecraft to determine where they are using what they can observe around them.

The FALCON payload is a joint flight experiment developed by NASA and EraDrive, a startup that emerged from Stanford University. The system combines EraDrive's Era-Core flight software and embedded algorithms with Starling's cameras and an onboard catalog of known satellites. Together, these tools enable GPS-independent navigation while also helping the spacecraft track activity and objects in its surroundings.

"FALCON is yet another success for the Starling demonstration mission. The results from FALCON can have far-reaching implications for on-orbit space-traffic monitoring, collision avoidance, and alternative navigation," said Roger Hunter, program manager for NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program at NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley. "The number of 'firsts' from Starling just keeps growing."

Using Satellites and Debris as Reference Points

The FALCON demonstration evaluated two related capabilities using Starling's onboard star tracker cameras. These cameras are standard spacecraft instruments that detect bright objects in space and help determine a spacecraft's orientation and position.

During position, navigation, and timing experiments, FALCON identified objects seen by Starling's cameras, including other spacecraft and orbital debris, and compared them with a publicly available catalog of known space objects maintained by the U.S. Department of War. Once the observed objects were identified and verified, FALCON used them as reference points to calculate Starling's orbit.

A separate set of experiments tested whether the spacecraft could improve orbital estimates for the objects it observed. Mission controllers loaded a catalog containing approximately 20,000 space objects and their predicted orbits onto Starling.

FALCON then compared the catalog information with observations gathered by Starling's cameras. Using those measurements, it calculated Starling's position while also refining the estimated locations of other objects in space. The resulting estimates were even more precise than the existing catalog data.

Over a period of three days, FALCON improved the known orbits of more than 200 objects without any intervention from operators on the ground.

A First for Autonomous Optical Navigation

FALCON's ability to determine its own orbit represents a first for a spacecraft navigating with optical cameras based on its position relative to other objects in space. In separate catalog updating tests, Starling also generated better onboard predictions of object positions than those supplied by ground stations.

These abilities could be especially valuable for future networks of satellites operating without GPS. Coordinated satellite systems may play an important role in supporting human activities on the surfaces of the Moon or Mars.

Precise spacecraft positioning is also essential for distributed science missions, where measurements collected from several locations in space must be accurately aligned. For space traffic management, autonomous navigation and onboard catalog updates could reduce dependence on ground-based tracking networks while improving collision avoidance.

From University Research to Commercial Technology

The FALCON experiment also demonstrates NASA's role in helping university research develop into commercial technology. The work began as a University SmallSat Technology Partnerships project before developing into EraDrive, which is now commercializing its Era-Core software and related hardware for wider use.

Starling gave the technology an opportunity to be tested under real conditions in orbit, demonstrating how advanced flight software can allow satellites to function as increasingly autonomous navigators.

Later this year, Starling, which launched in 2023, will expand the FALCON experiment using Era-Core. The mission's four spacecraft will share tracking information with one another and use those combined observations to refine their positions collectively.

NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley leads the Starling mission. NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program, based at Ames and within the Research and Technology Mission Directorate, funds and manages the Starling mission.

Materials provided by NASA. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Everybody wants a piece of Jensen these days.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The baying of the hounds can be heard at a distance. Yesterday, Nvidia's Jensen Huang confirmed that it was indeed true that he had a romantic affair with Alice Huang (not related to the family), who U.S. intelligence services claim is a Chinese honeypot tasked with facilitating smuggling of the firm's advanced GPUs into China. Now, a class-action lawsuit and investigations have started flying from almost everywhere.

This development is not unexpected, as basic securities law allows shareholders to sue their investees if they misrepresent facts or otherwise perform actions that cause a drop in stock prices. They may have a point, as Nvidia's shares dipped significantly when the confirmation of Jensen's infidelity and the ramifications thereof hit the press everywhere.

The legal proceedings are bound to be numerous, but as of right now, the first class-action lawsuit was filed by the Rosen Law Firm. The suit claims that Nvidia and Jensen Huang failed to disclose or misled investors regarding Jensen's affair with Alice, lied about it when asked, raised national security concerns, and caused a subsequent loss of Nvidia's reputation. You can check out the case docket at PacerMonitor here.

While legal analysis is beyond the scope of this news, Nvidia did downplay Jensen's relationship with Alice, saying that "Megaspeed has been no different than many other regional cloud customers. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year."

Other legal firms have launched their own investigations into the recent development, with essentially the same justification: Schall, Brown & Schwartz attributes a 5.5% pre-market-trading drop to Nvidia, as does Levi & Korsinsky. The office of Frank R. Cruz mentions an 8.8% figure related to intra-day exchanging, and is interested in shareholders with $50,000 in losses. The percentage-drop figure is 5.88% premarket for Bronstein, Gewritz & Grossman.

Every investigation and suit mentioned here requires no upfront fees from complainants, as with most class-action lawsuits. The underlying condition is often that the law firms involved get a cut of whichever damages or settlements may appear at the end of the lawsuit. Some of them, like the one by Levi & Korsinsky, even aim to relieve investors who've already sold their Nvidia shares and sold them at a loss, with no minimum amount required.

#10550. AAWireless expands beyond Android Auto adapters with new cooled Qi2 car charger

AAWireless is known for helping you convert a wired Android Auto connection into a wireless one, but the company is now launching a new product – an active-cooled Qi2 car charger.

Update 11/5: After debuting solely on the company’s website, AAWireless Charge is also now available on Amazon in the US.

“AAWireless Charge” is a Qi2 25W wireless car charger that costs $50. The charger is compatible with MagSafe, Pixelsnap, and Qi2 in general, and supports up to 25W charging speeds on compatible phones.

“Charge” also includes active cooling support with a built-in fan that the company says is “quiet” when active.

Included in the box you’ll find both an air vent mount as well as an adhesive base. There are also two magnetic ring adapters in the box for “Qi2 Ready” phones that don’t have magnets built-in or a magnetic case available. AAWireless says to use your car’s 12V outlet with the included adapter for “best performance,” but you can also use existing USB-C connections so long as they deliver enough power.

To be clear, there’s no wireless adapter built into the charger (as cool as that would be), but you can use this alongside an AAWireless adapter while driving, the company specifically mentions.

“AAWireless Charge” is available from the company’s website and is also on Amazon.

#10547. IBM quantum computer solves classically intractable problem in 15 minutes

Scientists used a new error correction strategy to encode 70 logical qubits and tackle a problem considered classically intractable.

The quantum computation was completed in about 15 minutes, while leading classical computing methods would require an impractical amount of time to perform the same task.

IBM and researchers at the University of Chicago have announced a quantum computing demonstration that meets key criteria for quantum advantage. The experiment performed a calculation beyond the practical reach of leading classical simulation techniques while also providing evidence that the quantum computer produced a reliable result.

In a new paper, "Sampling hard circuits with verifiably high fidelity," the researchers describe how they achieved both goals using a newly designed form of encoded quantum circuit. The work represents one of the largest demonstrations of logical quantum computing reported so far.

The circuits and experimental results have also been made publicly available through the Quantum Advantage Tracker.

Why Quantum Results Are So Difficult to Verify

Researchers have long used a benchmark called random circuit sampling (RCS) to explore whether quantum computers can outperform conventional machines.

In simplified terms, RCS challenges a quantum computer to generate patterns that become so complicated that a classical computer cannot efficiently reproduce them. That makes the benchmark useful for testing the limits of classical simulation.

But it also creates a major problem. Once the quantum calculation becomes too difficult for a classical computer to reproduce, verifying that the quantum machine actually produced the correct result becomes increasingly difficult as well. Eventually, checking the answer can itself become infeasible unless researchers make strong assumptions about how the quantum computer behaves internally.

The IBM and University of Chicago team approached this verification problem by developing a more structured alternative to RCS.

The researchers showed that their method preserves the same computational hardness criteria associated with RCS, meaning the problem remains extremely difficult for classical computers. At the same time, the added structure allows errors to be detected during the quantum computation.

"Verification remains one of the biggest challenges in firmly establishing experimental quantum advantage," said Bill Fefferman, Associate Professor at the University of Chicago. "This experiment develops techniques to better characterize the fidelity of hard quantum states under noise, increasing confidence that the quantum computer is solving a computationally hard problem."

Soumik Ghosh, PhD student in Fefferman's group at the University of Chicago, added, "Beyond strengthening experimental validation, advances in verification have the potential to unlock practical applications for the next generation of quantum computers."

The experiment also included one of the world's largest-known demonstrations of quantum error correction.

The researchers operated 70 logical qubits. Unlike individual physical qubits, logical qubits are encoded in a way that helps protect quantum information from errors and noise.

Using those logical qubits, the team carried out 2,415 logical two-qubit operations and 468 logical "T gates," two measures that reflect the complexity of the quantum circuit.

The encoded design significantly improved reliability. Effective logical error rates were 10 times lower than the underlying physical error rates, allowing the circuit to maintain unusually high fidelity even while performing a large number of quantum operations.

IBM Says Quantum Advantage Has Entered a New Stage

"We are now firmly in the quantum advantage era," said Jay Gambetta, Director of IBM Research and IBM Fellow. "We have demonstrated a quantum computation beyond the practical reach of classical computers that establishes, with statistical confidence, a lower bound on how faithfully it was executed. This milestone gives scientists, developers, and businesses a new foundation for trusting quantum computers as they scale to problems far beyond what we can achieve classically."

The researchers found that many leading classical simulation methods would face prohibitive runtimes when attempting the same task.

The IBM quantum computer, by comparison, completed the computation in approximately 15 minutes.

A Step Toward Larger, More Trustworthy Quantum Computers

Speed alone is not enough for quantum computing to become useful at larger scales. Researchers also need ways to suppress errors and establish confidence that a quantum system produced a valid result.

This experiment advances both goals at once by combining large-scale logical quantum computing with a method for evaluating the reliability of a calculation that is already beyond practical classical simulation.

Error correction and trustworthy verification are considered essential for scaling quantum computers toward more difficult problems, making the new demonstration an important step toward that goal.

Materials provided by IBM. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10546. Orbiting ESA satellite plays Doom after receiving voice commands from amateur radio operators on Earth

An Oslo rooftop, about 80 watts, and a hand-aimed antenna were enough to reach orbit.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An Oslo radio amateur group, working with Tanagra Space, got a European Space Agency satellite to recognize and launch the 1993 game Doom after catching the word in a voice command sent over the 23 cm band. It took seven attempts, shared with a group in Poland, and the command finally got through on the last two, on the same day in late July. The satellite “understood its assignment,” with the help of a matcher after the 27 MB onboard speech model transcribed the audio, returning a postcard with details of the launch.

The satellite, named OPS-SAT PRETTY and flying under the ESA’s OPS-SAT Space Lab program for outside experiments, ran the game in an experiment punningly called PRETTY DOOMed. Doom ran on a different satellite, the OPS-SAT-1, in 2023, but this time is different, as the game was launched with a voice command.

The radio club sending the command in question is LA4O, of the Norwegian Radio Relay League, hams transmitting as part of the experiment. The club’s signal went over 1296 MHz narrowband FM at about 80 W, indicated, using an Icom IC-9700 radio and a Wimo helix antenna at 15.1 dBi, directed by hand. As for the voice, Run 6, the morning pass, used a looped synthesized voice from a keyer, with three of six captures detecting the command. Run 7, the evening pass, used a live operator at a microphone, with one of five usable captures detecting it.

The live operator’s command was “LIMA ALFA FOUR OSCAR, PRETTY PLAY DOOM DOOM DOOM, PRETTY PLAY DOOM DOOM DOOM.” The call sign is for legal identification.

On board the satellite, the voice is transcribed by a 27 MB quantized English speech-recognition model, picked because it could fit the satellite’s limited computer. The voice was handled by software-defined radio (SDR), which was able to narrow in on the voice and demodulate the source for the speech model. Tuning and demodulating a signal is usually handled by fixed circuits, but using software has the advantage of being mutable with an uploaded update. The voice used this time “was only the test signal,” project lead Georges Labrèche said, and the same setup could handle other things with a new model.

The live-voice transcript caught “AND FAR BRIEFLY PLAYING DOOM STEAM DOOM PRECIPATED” with two exact DOOM matches, although one DOOM is enough. Morning detections passed only on fuzzy tolerance with transcripts such as “PLAY DOON.” The achievement is less on the understood side and more about differentiating a voice and a specific command out of the noise in orbit.

This can be an iffy business, which is one reason it took seven runs, and being able to update proved useful. The spacecraft heard LA4O, but the output was mostly static, because about 170 kHz reached the FM discriminator for a signal of about ±5 kHz. Offline analysis brought a fix: find the peak within ±100 kHz, shift to baseband, low-pass, and decimate before demodulation. It’s effectively like tuning a car radio to a specific station.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Tanagra then reproduced the fix and was able to remove a redundant audio filter. Delivery of the fix took about a week, although the Oslo group’s effort took 59 days and about 7,200 NOK (about $770), pizza included.

Tanagra proposes repeat runs that keep the original data and send it back down to Earth, so the researchers can test the live-voice result properly. Better detection may be possible with larger speech models, too, which the team could test on the ground first. Labrèche says the team has demonstrated “a satellite that can listen to any type of radio signal.” Hopefully, the setup can one day detect “mayday” calls and even radio bursts from space.
